  \item \textbf{Race and ethnicity.} Correlate with both health-care access/coding and behavioral
  patterns; collapsed to protect small cells while retaining the major contrasts.
  \item \textbf{Education and income.} Socio-economic status shapes device access, health-care
  engagement (hence diagnosis probability), and lifestyle; both are carried with an explicit
  Unknown/Missing level.
  \item \textbf{Smoking.} Nicotine lowers lower-esophageal-sphincter tone (a direct reflux mechanism)
  and degrades sleep; a classic confounder of any sleep--reflux association.
  \item \textbf{Alcohol.} Relaxes the sphincter, disrupts sleep architecture, and associates with
  activity; modeled on the daily-quantity Likert scale.
  \item \textbf{Median BMI.} The single most important confounder: adiposity raises intra-abdominal
  pressure and promotes reflux and hiatal herniation while also shortening sleep and reducing activity.
  \item \textbf{Charlson Comorbidity Index.} Captures residual multimorbidity that drives both
  health-care contact (and thus coding) and reduced sleep/activity; categorical by default.
  \item \textbf{Depression and anxiety.} Alter sleep, elevate visceral symptom reporting, and associate
  with reflux diagnosis; adjusted directly rather than folded into the Charlson index.
  \item \textbf{Peptic ulcer disease.} Shares acid pathophysiology and PPI treatment with GERD; a GERD-
  specific covariate absent from the antecedent IBS study.
  \item \textbf{Irritable bowel syndrome.} Frequently overlaps GERD and disturbs sleep; entered as a
  covariate (not the outcome), rebuilt from the condition table under the same temporal rule.
  \item \textbf{Diabetes, hypertension, heart failure, MI, stroke, COPD, sleep apnea.} Cardiometabolic
  and respiratory comorbidities that pattern both physiology and health-care coding; sleep apnea in
  particular is strongly linked to both disturbed sleep and reflux.
  \item \textbf{Medication-use flags.} Beta-blockers, calcium-channel blockers, stimulants,
  antidepressants, antipsychotics, anxiolytics, and hypnotics all affect measured sleep and heart rate
  and may mark conditions linked to reflux; each is a $\geq 2$-date pre-exposure flag.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Acid-suppression therapy is deliberately \emph{absent} from this list: as a probable mediator
(downstream of reflux), it is used only to define the treated-GERD sensitivity outcome, never as an
adjustment covariate.

%======================================================================
\section{Software Architecture}
\label{sec:arch}
%======================================================================
The implementation is deliberately layered so that all six configurations share one statistical engine
and one data-loading path. Only three things differ between configurations: the cohort, the exposure
list, and which outcome column the driver stratifies on. Everything else --- estimator, covariate set,
reference levels, missing-data handling, table format --- is defined exactly once.

\subsection{The four layers}
\begin{table}[h]\centering\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{4.9cm}p{8.7cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{File} & \textbf{Layer and responsibility} \\
\midrule
\code{GERD Outcome Data Upload R9.R} & \textbf{Acquisition.} BigQuery pull of GERD condition records
with Cohort Builder descendant expansion, previewing both the captured concepts and the oesophagitis
subset. Produces \code{R9_gerd_outcome.rds}. Run once. \\
\addlinespace
\code{GERD Data Prep R9.R} & \textbf{Data layer.} Schema-aware loading of cohort, exposures and the
one-row-per-participant covariate bundle. Implements prefer-pre-built-else-derive, concept-ID mapping
for socio-economic variables, NULL-safe joins, column slimming against the join hazards, and memory
release after large tables are reduced. \\
\addlinespace
\code{GERD Analysis Helpers R9.R} & \textbf{Engine.} Outcome definitions and labels, exposure lists,
adjustment set, master label lookup; quartile cutoff labelling; descriptive, univariable and
multivariable routines; the separation guard; assumption diagnostics; and the manuscript tables and
figures. \\
\addlinespace
\code{Sleep and GERD Analysis File R9.R} & \textbf{Driver.} Configurations C1 and C2. \\
\code{Activity and GERD Analysis File R9.R} & \textbf{Driver.} Configurations C3 and C4. \\
\code{Activity Sleep and GERD Analysis File R9.R} & \textbf{Driver.} Configurations C5 and C6, plus
the mutually-adjusted sleep$\times$activity pair models. \\
\addlinespace
\code{GERD Pharmacologics Data Upload.R} & \textbf{Optional acquisition.} PPI (21600095) and
$H_2$RA (21600096) exposures for the treated-GERD sensitivity analysis. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Code layers. Each driver sources the data layer and the engine, then delegates.}
\label{tab:layers}
\end{table}

\subsection{What a driver actually contains}
Because the two shared files carry the logic, a driver reduces to a short, readable sequence: load the
outcome source, build both phenotypes, load covariates, assemble one row per participant, create
integer quartiles, and hand off to the engine.

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={The essential structure of every driver.}]
source("GERD Analysis Helpers R9.R")   # engine
source("GERD Data Prep R9.R")          # data layer

R9_gerd_outcome        <- read_first_existing("R9_gerd_outcome.rds", required = TRUE)
R9_gerd_outcome_status <- build_gerd_outcomes(R9_gerd_outcome,
                            first_fitbit_sleep_date_df, "first_fitbit_sleep_date")
covars_df              <- gerd_load_covariates(anchor = "sleep",
                            fitbit_dates = first_fitbit_sleep_date_df,
                            date_col = "first_fitbit_sleep_date")

final_analysis_sleep_gerd_df <- valid_population_sleep %>%
  inner_join(sleep_summary_filtered, by = "person_id") %>%
  left_join(covars_df, by = "person_id") %>%
  left_join(R9_gerd_outcome_status, by = "person_id") %>%
  apply_primary_outcome_def() %>%            # selects the primary case definition
  mutate(min_asleep_quartile = ntile(avg_min_asleep, 4), ...)

modeling_df   <- prep_modeling_df(final_analysis_sleep_gerd_df, SLEEP_EXPOSURES)
sleep_results <- analyze_all_outcomes(modeling_df, exposures = SLEEP_EXPOSURES,
                                      coverage_var = "n_valid_nights", stub = "sleep")
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{The data layer in detail}
\code{GERD Data Prep R9.R} exposes a small number of functions with clearly separated concerns.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item \code{read_first_existing(candidates, label, required)} --- loads the first file that exists
  from a candidate vector, logging which one it used. This is the mechanism behind
  prefer-pre-built-else-derive, and it makes provenance explicit in the run log.
  \item \code{build_gerd_outcomes(pull, fitbit_dates, date_col)} --- derives all four outcome columns
  (\code{has_gerd_any_ever}, \code{has_gerd_any_post_fitbit}, and the two oesophagitis equivalents) from the
  single pull, and prints the case counts under each rule.
  \item \code{gerd_load_covariates(anchor, ...)} --- assembles the covariate bundle for the sleep or
  activity anchor, applying the column-slimming rules of Section~\ref{sec:joinhazards} and coalescing
  binary EHR indicators to \code{FALSE}.
  \item \code{map_education()} / \code{map_income()} --- concept-ID mapping with text fallback.
  \item \code{derive_comorbidities()}, \code{derive_medications()}, \code{derive_bmi()},
  \code{derive_smoking()}, \code{derive_alcohol()}, \code{derive_ibs_covariate()} --- the
  re-derivation fallbacks, used only when a pre-built object is missing.
  \item \code{drop_big()} --- removes named large objects and triggers garbage collection, used after
  each multi-gigabyte table has been reduced to its summary.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Two defensive details are worth noting. Joins in the covariate assembler use a NULL-safe
wrapper, so a missing covariate block degrades to ``that covariate is absent'' rather than aborting
the run. And the assembler guarantees that \code{has_pud} and \code{has_ibs} exist in the output even
if no source supplied them, setting them to \code{FALSE} with an explicit message --- which the
separation guard will then correctly identify as single-level and drop from the models.

\subsection{The engine in detail}
\code{GERD Analysis Helpers R9.R} holds every modelling decision in one place.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item \textbf{Configuration constants} --- \code{GERD_PRIMARY_DEF} (default \code{"post_fitbit"}),
  \code{GERD_POST_FITBIT_LAG_DAYS} (180), \code{GERD_P_ADJUST} (\code{"bonferroni"}),
  \code{GERD_MIN_CELL} (1), \code{GERD_OUTPUT_DIR}, and the descriptive test choices.
  \item \code{GERD_OUTCOMES} --- the two outcomes and their factor labels, driving the both-outcome loop.
  \item \code{SLEEP_EXPOSURES}, \code{ACTIVITY_EXPOSURES}, \code{GERD_ADJ_COVARS},
  \code{GERD_LABELS}, and the exposure-to-source and exposure-to-unit maps used by Table~2.
  \item \code{apply_primary_outcome_def()} --- selects which case definition becomes primary and
  retains the other as \code{*_sens}.
  \item \code{prep_modeling_df()} --- builds collapsed factor covariates and relevels quartiles to
  Q1--Q4. Deliberately \emph{defensive}: each collapsed variable is constructed only if it is not
  already present and its source column exists, so the same function works whether the frame arrived
  from pre-built objects (which already supply \code{education_collapsed}) or from re-derivation.
  \item \code{make_quartile_labels()} --- computes the numeric boundaries and renders the
  \code{Q1 (<368)} style labels required by the target Table~2, with per-metric scaling (sleep
  efficiency is reported as a percentage) and thousands separators for step counts.
  \item \code{drop_unstable_covariates()} --- the separation guard (Section~\ref{sec:guard}).
  \item \code{run_models_for_outcome()}, \code{manuscript_table1/2()},
  \code{manuscript_supp_univariate/multivariable()}, \code{forest_plot_exposures()},
  \code{gvif_plot()}, \code{diagnostics_summary()}.
  \item \code{analyze_all_outcomes()} --- the driver-facing entry point that loops both outcomes and
  emits the full manuscript set.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{A note on package attachment}
The engine installs but deliberately does \emph{not} attach \code{MASS}, \code{car} and \code{pROC},
referencing them as \code{MASS::stepAIC}, \code{car::vif} and \code{pROC::roc}. The reason is
concrete rather than stylistic: \code{MASS::select()} masks \code{dplyr::select()}, and because the
engine is sourced before any data manipulation, attaching MASS breaks every subsequent \code{select()}
call in the pipeline. The tidyverse packages are attached with \code{dplyr} last so its verbs take
precedence. This defect was observed in practice (Section~\ref{sec:defects}).

%======================================================================
\section{The Shared Modeling Engine and Descriptive Statistics}
%======================================================================

\subsection{Engine functions}
Every configuration is executed by \code{GERD Analysis Helpers R9.R}. Its exported objects and
functions are:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \code{GERD_OUTCOMES} --- the two outcomes and their factor labels; \code{SLEEP_EXPOSURES} and
  \code{ACTIVITY_EXPOSURES}; \code{GERD_ADJ_COVARS}; and
  \code{GERD_LABELS} (master labels).
  \item \code{prep_modeling_df(df, exposures)} --- builds collapsed factor covariates (race, ethnicity,
  sex, education, income, alcohol, smoking, Charlson category) and relevels each quartile exposure to
  Q1$\rightarrow$Q4 with Q1 reference.
  \item \code{descriptive_table()}, \code{univariate_table()}, \code{run_models_for_outcome()},
  \code{stack_results()}, \code{run_diagnostics()}.
  \item \code{analyze_all_outcomes(modeling_df, exposures)} --- the driver that loops \emph{both}
  outcomes, producing descriptive, univariable, multivariable, and diagnostic output for each.
\end{itemize}
Each analysis file builds its one-row-per-participant frame, creates integer quartiles, calls
\code{prep_modeling_df()}, then \code{analyze_all_outcomes()}. Centralization guarantees that the sleep,
activity, and combined analyses --- and both outcomes within each --- are estimated by identical code.

\subsection{Descriptive statistics}
For each outcome a \code{gtsummary::tbl_summary} table is stratified by the outcome (labeled
``No GERD''/``GERD'' or ``No oesophagitis''/``Oesophagitis''), reporting the exposure quartiles,
continuous exposure metrics, demographics, behavioral and socio-economic factors, the Charlson index,
all comorbidities, PUD, IBS, medications, and median BMI. Chi-square tests are attached for the
collapsed categorical demographics, Bonferroni-adjusted $p$-values are added, and emphasis is placed
on $q$ rather than raw $p$. Race, ethnicity, sex, education, and income are collapsed to protect small
cells and improve legibility.

%======================================================================
\section{Reporting Conventions and Output Structure}
%======================================================================
Each configuration produces a fixed set of outputs in a fixed order, so that the six configurations can
be read side by side. This section documents that structure.

\subsection{Per-configuration outputs}
For each outcome the engine emits, in order: (1) a stratified descriptive table
(\code{tbl_summary}) with counts/percentages for categorical variables, medians/IQRs for continuous
variables, a Missing row where applicable, chi-square tests for the collapsed demographics, and adjusted
$q$-values; (2) a univariable table (\code{tbl_uvregression}) of unadjusted ORs with 95\% CIs for every
exposure and covariate; (3) a stacked multivariable table (\code{tbl_stack}) with, per exposure, a full
model and a backward model side by side (\code{tbl_merge}), each footnoted with its complete-case $N$;
and (4) a console diagnostics block. The combined file additionally emits the mutually-adjusted
sleep$\times$activity tables.

\subsection{Numbers and rounding}
Odds ratios and confidence bounds are reported to two decimal places; percentages to one; the AUC to
three. Cell sizes below the \emph{All of Us} small-count reporting threshold must be suppressed or
combined before any output leaves the workspace --- a reason the demographic categories are collapsed
in \code{prep_modeling_df()}.

\subsection{What is fixed versus what varies}
Across the six configurations the estimator, covariate set, reference levels, missing-data handling,
and table format are \emph{fixed} by the shared helper. Only three things \emph{vary}: the cohort (which
eligibility gate applies), the exposure list handed to the engine, and the outcome column the driver
stratifies on. This separation is what licenses direct comparison of, say, the sleep effect on GERD (C1)
with the sleep effect on oesophagitis (C2) or the activity effect on GERD (C3).

%======================================================================
\section{Manuscript Output Specification}
\label{sec:outputs}
%======================================================================
The analysis does not emit exploratory objects for later reshaping; it emits the manuscript's tables
and figures directly. Running any driver writes, for \emph{both} outcomes, a fixed set of files into
\code{./manuscript_output/}, prefixed by exposure set (\code{sleep_}, \code{activity_},
\code{combined_}) and then by outcome (\code{gerd_any_}, \code{esophagitis_}).

\begin{table}[h]\centering\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{6.0cm}p{7.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{File} & \textbf{Manuscript element} \\
\midrule
\code{*_table1.csv} & \textbf{Table 1} --- demographics and characteristics by outcome group, as
median [IQR] for continuous variables and $n$ (\%) for categorical, with a single $p$-value column \\
\addlinespace
\code{*_table2.csv} & \textbf{Table 2} --- exposure metrics by outcome group, each participant-level
average immediately followed by its quartiles, with the numeric cutoffs printed in the row labels \\
\addlinespace
\code{*_supp_table1_univariate.csv} & \textbf{Supplement Table 1} --- univariable logistic regression:
$N$, OR, 95\% CI, $p$, adjusted $p$; reference levels rendered as ``--'' \\
\addlinespace
\code{*_supp_table2_multivariable.csv} & \textbf{Supplement Table 2} --- multivariable models, exposure
rows only, one model per metric, with per-model $N$ \\
\addlinespace
\code{*_figure1_forest.png} & \textbf{Figure 1} --- forest plots of adjusted odds ratios, faceted by
metric, Q1 as reference, log-scaled axis with a null line at 1 \\
\addlinespace
\code{*_figure2_gvif.png} & \textbf{Figure 2} --- adjusted GVIF bar chart with the conventional
threshold marked \\
\addlinespace
\code{*_diagnostics.csv} & The numbers quoted in the Results prose: adjusted GVIF range, maximum
Cook's distance, Box--Tidwell $p$-values for BMI and age, AUC range, duplicate-identifier count \\
\addlinespace
\code{*_quartile_cutoffs.csv} & Exact unrounded quartile boundaries, for the Table~2 footnote stating
that displayed cutoffs are rounded but exact values were used in analysis \\
\addlinespace
\code{combined_*_mutually_adjusted_pairs.csv} & Sleep$\times$activity pair estimates (C5/C6) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Emitted files and the manuscript elements they populate.}
\end{table}

\subsection{Quartile cutoff labelling}
The target Table~2 does not merely report quartile membership; it reports the numeric boundary of each
quartile in the row label, for example \code{Q2 (368-399)}. The engine computes these from the data
rather than requiring them to be transcribed. Boundaries are taken from the empirical quartiles of the
underlying continuous metric, rounded for display while the unrounded values are exported separately.
Two per-metric refinements are applied: sleep efficiency is a proportion in the data but is reported
as a percentage, so it is scaled before labelling; and step counts receive thousands separators.

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={Table 2 as produced by the code (excerpt, synthetic data).}]
Number of valid nights                     886 [532, 1,243]
Average sleeping minutes                     398 [369, 430]
Minutes asleep quartiles (minutes)
    Q1 (<369)                                    702 (25%)
    Q2 (369-398)                                 705 (25%)
    Q3 (399-430)                                 692 (25%)
    Q4 (>430)                                    693 (25%)
Average in bed minutes                       453 [420, 487]
Minutes in bed quartiles (minutes)
    Q1 (<421)                                    700 (25%)
    ...
Sleep efficiency                       0.885 [0.866, 0.905]
Sleep efficiency quartiles (%)
    Q2 (87-88)      Q3 (89-91)      Q4 (>91)
Daily steps quartiles (steps)
    Q1 (<5,785)     Q2 (5,785-7,489)     Q3 (7,490-9,108)     Q4 (>9,108)
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent The structure --- coverage count, then for each metric its average followed by its labelled
quartiles --- reproduces the antecedent paper's Table~2 layout exactly, so the output can be
transferred with formatting rather than restructuring.

\subsection{Multiplicity in the emitted tables}
Both supplement tables carry an adjusted $p$-value column alongside the raw one. The adjustment
follows \code{GERD_P_ADJUST}, which defaults to Bonferroni across the models within an outcome,
matching the antecedent paper. Setting it to \code{"fdr"} switches to Benjamini--Hochberg
$q$-values without any other change.

%======================================================================
\section{Statistical Preliminaries and Notation}
%======================================================================
This section fixes the notation and the elementary quantities used throughout, so that later sections
can refer to them without ambiguity.

\subsection{Odds, odds ratios, and the logit}
For a binary outcome $Y \in \{0,1\}$ with $p = \Pr[Y=1]$, the \emph{odds} are $p/(1-p)$ and the
\emph{logit} (log-odds) is $\logit(p) = \log\{p/(1-p)\}$, a monotone map from $(0,1)$ to
$(-\infty,\infty)$ that makes a linear predictor sensible. The \emph{odds ratio} (OR) comparing two
covariate settings is the ratio of their odds; an OR of $1$ denotes no association, $<1$ lower odds,
and $>1$ higher odds. In a logistic model each coefficient $\beta$ satisfies $e^{\beta} = \text{OR}$ per
unit change in its covariate, holding the others fixed; for a quartile contrast $e^{\beta_j}$ is the OR
of quartile $j$ versus the Q1 reference.

\subsection{The model and its likelihood}
With covariate vector $x_i$ and parameter vector $\theta = (\beta_0, \boldsymbol\beta, \boldsymbol\gamma)$,
\[
p_i(\theta) = \frac{1}{1 + \exp(-x_i^\top \theta)}, \qquad
\ell(\theta) = \sum_{i=1}^{N}\big\{ y_i \log p_i(\theta) + (1-y_i)\log(1-p_i(\theta)) \big\}.
\]
The maximum-likelihood estimate $\hat\theta$ maximizes $\ell$; R's \code{glm} solves the score equations
by iteratively reweighted least squares. Standard errors come from the inverse observed information,
and a Wald 95\% confidence interval for an OR is $\exp(\hat\beta \pm 1.96\,\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}(\hat\beta))$.
Confidence intervals are reported on the OR scale throughout.

\subsection{Discrimination, calibration, and fit}
Model \emph{discrimination} --- the ability to rank cases above non-cases --- is summarized by the area
under the receiver-operating-characteristic curve (AUC, equivalently the concordance $c$-statistic),
computed by \code{pROC}; $0.5$ is chance and $1.0$ is perfect. Because the outcomes are relatively rare,
AUC is expected to be modest and is used comparatively (full vs backward model, across exposures) rather
than as an absolute performance claim. Nested models are compared by AIC during backward selection.
Separation and sparsity are monitored through coefficient standard errors, as described in the
diagnostics.

\subsection{Reference levels and contrasts}
All quartile exposures use Q1 (lowest) as the reference; all categorical covariates use an explicit,
clinically natural reference (\code{"<30"} for age, \code{"Non-Hispanic"} for ethnicity, \code{"0"} for
the Charlson category, \code{"Non-smoker"} for smoking). Fixing references in one place --- the shared
helper's \code{prep_modeling_df()} --- guarantees that every configuration reports contrasts against the
same baseline, so ORs are comparable across the sleep, activity, and combined analyses.

%======================================================================
\section{Univariable and Multivariable Analysis}
%======================================================================

\subsection{Univariable models}
Each candidate predictor is regressed on the outcome individually via
\code{gtsummary::tbl_uvregression} with \code{family = binomial} and exponentiated coefficients,
yielding unadjusted odds ratios with 95\% CIs for every exposure and covariate, including the
GERD-specific \code{has_pud}, \code{has_sleep_apnea}, \code{on_sleep_med} and \code{on_narcotic}
terms.

\subsection{Multivariable models}
Because the exposures within a set are collinear, a \emph{separate} adjusted model is fit for each. For
exposure $X$ (a quartile factor with reference Q1) and covariate vector $\mathbf{Z}$,
\[
\logit\!\big(\Pr[Y=1]\big) = \beta_0 + \beta_2 \ind[X{=}Q_2] + \beta_3 \ind[X{=}Q_3]
+ \beta_4 \ind[X{=}Q_4] + \sum_{k} \gamma_k Z_k ,
\]
so $e^{\beta_j}$ is the adjusted odds ratio for quartile $j$ versus Q1. The adjustment set is
\begin{center}
\code{age_cat, sex, race, ethnicity, education, income, alcohol, smoking, median_bmi,}\\
\code{has_sleep_apnea, on_sleep_med, on_narcotic, has_depression, has_anxiety,}\\
\code{has_diabetes, has_hypertension, has_pud}.
\end{center}
For each exposure the engine fits a \emph{full} model and a \emph{backward} model selected by AIC
(\code{MASS::stepAIC}) in which the exposure is forced to remain (the selection lower scope is
\code{\textasciitilde{} exposure}). Each model reports the complete-case $N$, the ROC AUC via
\code{pROC}, and variance inflation factors via \code{car}; results are rendered as merged
full-vs-backward tables and stacked across exposures. All six configurations reuse this exact procedure.

\subsection{Combined mutually-adjusted models}
The combined file additionally fits the mutually-adjusted sleep$+$activity models of
Section~6.3, so that, for each outcome, the sleep and activity contributions are estimated in a single
equation and each is adjusted for the other domain plus the full covariate set.

\subsection{Assumption diagnostics}
A dedicated diagnostics routine interrogates: \textbf{(1)} independence (no duplicate
\code{person_id}); \textbf{(2)} multicollinearity (generalized VIF $>2$ or VIF $>5$); \textbf{(3)}
separation/sparsity (logit standard errors $>5$); \textbf{(4)} influential points (Cook's distance
against $4/N$); and \textbf{(5)} linearity in the logit for continuous covariates (Box--Tidwell $x\log
x$ term for median BMI). It runs for both outcomes of every configuration.

%======================================================================

\section{The Six Analysis Configurations in Detail}\label{sec:six-configs-ref}
%======================================================================
The design crosses two outcomes with three exposure sets. The six resulting configurations are
summarized in Table~\ref{tab:sixdetail} and described individually below. Configurations sharing a
column are produced by the same analysis file, because \code{analyze_all_outcomes()} loops both
outcomes over a single modeling data frame.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}c p{2.7cm} p{2.9cm} p{2.4cm} p{3.0cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{ID} & \textbf{Outcome} & \textbf{Exposure set} & \textbf{Cohort} & \textbf{File} \\
\midrule
C1 & Broad GERD & Sleep (8 quartiles) & $\geq 180$ nights & Sleep and GERD \\
C2 & Oesophagitis & Sleep (8 quartiles) & $\geq 180$ nights & Sleep and GERD \\
C3 & Broad GERD & Activity (7 quartiles) & $\geq 30$ days & Activity and GERD \\
C4 & Oesophagitis & Activity (7 quartiles) & $\geq 30$ days & Activity and GERD \\
C5 & Broad GERD & Combined (15 + pairs) & Intersection & Activity Sleep and GERD \\
C6 & Oesophagitis & Combined (15 + pairs) & Intersection & Activity Sleep and GERD \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The six analysis configurations in detail.}
\label{tab:sixdetail}
\end{table}

\subsection{C1 --- Sleep and broad GERD}
The reference configuration. On the $\geq 180$-night sleep cohort, each of the eight sleep quartiles is
regressed on \code{has_gerd_any} unadjusted and then adjusted for the full covariate set, with a
full-vs-backward pairing per exposure. This is the direct GERD analogue of the project's IBS sleep
study and the primary test of the ``better sleep, lower reflux odds'' hypothesis.

\subsection{C2 --- Sleep and oesophagitis}
Identical machinery, outcome \code{has_esophagitis}. Because the erosive subset is rarer, case counts
per quartile are smaller and confidence intervals wider; the separation diagnostic is especially
relevant. Directional agreement with C1 argues that any sleep association acts on mucosal injury and
not only on symptom reporting.

\subsection{C3 --- Activity and broad GERD}
On the $\geq 30$-valid-day activity cohort, the seven activity quartiles (steps, four activity-intensity
bands, sedentary minutes, and corrected max heart rate) are modeled against \code{has_gerd_any}. Sedentary
time and low step counts are hypothesized to associate with higher odds; the corrected max-HR exposure
provides a cardiovascular-fitness proxy.

\subsection{C4 --- Activity and oesophagitis}
The activity exposures against the erosive subset, again precision-limited by case count. Comparison
with C3 mirrors the C1/C2 contrast in the activity domain.

\subsection{C5 --- Combined exposures and broad GERD}
On the intersection cohort, all fifteen exposures are modeled separately (as in C1 and C3 but on the
common sample), and the four mutually-adjusted sleep$\times$activity pairs are fit so that each domain
is adjusted for the other. This isolates independent contributions: a sleep association that survives
adjustment for steps, and an activity association that survives adjustment for sleep duration, are more
credible than either single-domain estimate.

\subsection{C6 --- Combined exposures and oesophagitis}
The most demanding configuration: the erosive outcome on the (smallest) intersection cohort with
mutually-adjusted models. It is interpreted cautiously and primarily for consistency of direction with
C5, C2, and C4.

%======================================================================
\section{The Logistic Model in Depth}
%======================================================================
\subsection{Specification and estimation}
Each adjusted model is a binomial generalized linear model with the canonical logit link. Writing
$p_i = \Pr[Y_i = 1 \mid X_i, Z_i]$ for participant $i$, the model is
\[
\log\!\frac{p_i}{1-p_i} = \beta_0 + \sum_{j=2}^{4}\beta_j\,\ind[X_i = Q_j] + \sum_k \gamma_k Z_{ik},
\]
estimated by maximum likelihood via iteratively reweighted least squares (R's \code{glm}). The
quartile exposure enters as a three-parameter contrast against Q1, so the model makes no assumption of
linearity across quartiles; a $U$-shaped or threshold relationship is representable.

\subsection{Interpretation of the quartile contrasts}
The exponentiated coefficient $e^{\beta_j}$ is the odds ratio comparing quartile $j$ to Q1, adjusted
for all covariates. Reporting all three contrasts ($Q_2, Q_3, Q_4$ vs $Q_1$) reveals the \emph{shape}
of the exposure--outcome relationship rather than compressing it to a single slope. A monotone trend in
$e^{\beta_2}, e^{\beta_3}, e^{\beta_4}$ suggests dose--response; a non-monotone pattern (for example, an
elevated top and bottom quartile) suggests a non-linear or mixed-population effect worth further study.

\subsection{Variable selection and the backward model}
Alongside each full model, a backward-elimination model is selected by the Akaike Information Criterion
(\code{MASS::stepAIC}, \code{direction = "backward"}) with the exposure \emph{forced} to remain (the
lower scope is \code{\textasciitilde{} exposure}). The backward model is a parsimony check: if the
exposure's estimate is stable between the full and backward models, the association is not an artifact
of a particular covariate set. AIC balances fit against the number of parameters, discouraging
overfitting in the smaller (oesophagitis, combined) cohorts.

\subsection{Discrimination and multicollinearity}
Each model reports the area under the receiver-operating-characteristic curve (AUC) via \code{pROC} as
a discrimination summary, and generalized variance inflation factors (GVIF) via \code{car}. For factor
covariates the reported quantity is $\text{GVIF}^{1/(2\,df)}$, comparable across terms of differing
degrees of freedom; values above $2$ (or ordinary VIF above $5$) flag problematic collinearity. Because
the exposures within a set are correlated, they are deliberately \emph{not} entered together in the
single-exposure models; the combined mutually-adjusted models pair only one sleep and one activity
exposure at a time to keep collinearity manageable.

%======================================================================
\section{Model Assumptions and Remediation}
%======================================================================
The diagnostics routine is not merely descriptive; each check is tied to a concrete remediation should
it fail. Table~\ref{tab:assump} lists the assumption, the test, what a violation implies, and the
recommended response.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{2.9cm}p{3.0cm}p{3.4cm}p{3.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Assumption} & \textbf{Test} & \textbf{Violation implies} & \textbf{Remediation} \\
\midrule
Independence & Duplicate \code{person_id} check & Correlated observations, understated SEs & Deduplicate joins; one row per participant. \\
No severe multicollinearity & GVIF$^{1/(2df)} > 2$ / VIF $> 5$ & Unstable, inflated coefficients & Drop or combine collinear covariates; rely on the backward model. \\
No perfect separation & Logit SE $> 5$ & Non-identifiable coefficients & Collapse sparse categories; consider penalized (Firth) logistic regression. \\
No overly influential points & Cook's distance vs $4/N$ & A few rows drive the fit & Inspect flagged rows; sensitivity refit excluding them. \\
Linearity in the logit & Box--Tidwell $x\log x$ for BMI & Continuous covariate mis-specified & Categorize BMI or add a spline/quadratic term. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Logistic-regression assumptions, diagnostics, and remediation.}
\label{tab:assump}
\end{table}

\noindent The exposure quartiles are categorical and therefore exempt from the linearity-in-the-logit
requirement; only genuinely continuous covariates (median BMI, and Charlson score if used continuously)
are tested. The separation check is most consequential for the rarer oesophagitis and treated-GERD
outcomes, where a covariate level may perfectly predict non-case status; the routine surfaces this as
an abnormally large standard error rather than letting it pass silently.

%======================================================================
\section{Separation, Sparsity, and Model Stability}
\label{sec:guard}
%======================================================================
Two features of this study make naive model fitting genuinely dangerous, and together they justify an
explicit safeguard rather than a post-hoc diagnostic.

\subsection{The problem}
First, one covariate is extraordinarily rare. \code{has_pud} is recorded for \textbf{137 participants}
across the entire source population (Table~\ref{tab:prebuilt}) --- well under one percent of the
analytic cohort. Second, the erosive oesophagitis outcome is itself uncommon by construction, being a
the less common of the two phenotypes in most EHR cohorts. When a rare covariate meets a rare outcome, the
cross-tabulation acquires empty cells.

An empty cell is not a minor inconvenience. If no participant with peptic ulcer disease has
oesophagitis, the maximum-likelihood estimate of that coefficient diverges: the fitted odds ratio runs
to zero or infinity, its standard error inflates without bound, and because the coefficients are
estimated jointly the instability propagates into the exposure estimate that the study actually cares
about. Worse, \code{glm} will return such a model without error. The output looks like a result.

\subsection{The guard}
Before fitting, every model screens its own covariate set against its own complete-case data. A
covariate is removed \emph{from that model only} if it is constant or all-missing, if it has a single
level after dropping unused ones, or if any outcome$\times$level cell falls below
\code{GERD_MIN_CELL} (default 1, i.e.\ any empty cell). Each removal is reported with its reason, and
the retained and dropped sets are stored on the result object so they can be recovered later.

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={The guard, applied inside the per-exposure fitting function.}]
guard      <- drop_unstable_covariates(d, outcome_col, covars,
                                       context = paste0(outcome_col, " ~ ", exposure, ":"))
covars_use <- guard$keep     # constant / single-level / empty-cell terms removed
m_full     <- glm(as.formula(paste0(outcome_col, " ~ ",
                    paste(c(exposure, covars_use), collapse = " + "))),
                  data = d, family = binomial())
# ... backward AIC, AUC, VIF, Cook's distance as usual ...
list(..., covars_used = covars_use, covars_dropped = guard$dropped)
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Why drop rather than penalise}
Alternative treatments exist. Firth's penalised likelihood would retain the covariate and produce a
finite estimate; exact logistic regression would give valid inference in very sparse tables. Both are
defensible, and either could be adopted later. Dropping was chosen here for three reasons: it keeps
the estimator identical to the published IBS paper, so the two studies remain directly comparable; it
is transparent, in that the reader can see precisely which term was removed and why; and the removed
terms are nuisance covariates rather than the exposure of interest, which is never dropped. If a
future reviewer prefers penalisation, the guard is a single function and the substitution is local.

\subsection{Observed behaviour}
In end-to-end validation the guard behaved exactly as designed. On the broad GERD outcome, with
several hundred cases, it did not fire: all covariates were retained. On the rare oesophagitis
outcome it fired on every exposure model, removing \code{has_pud} together with several sparse
demographic factors, and reported each removal. Without it those models would have returned
uninterpretable odds ratios with no indication that anything was wrong.

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={Console output from the guard during validation (abridged).}]
[sparsity guard] has_esophagitis ~ min_asleep_quartile: dropped 5 covariate(s):
  age_cat (empty cell (min=0)); sex_birth_collapsed (empty cell (min=0));
  race_collapsed (empty cell (min=0)); ethnicity_collapsed (empty cell (min=0));
  has_pud (empty cell (min=0))
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Reporting obligation}
Because the guard changes the fitted model, its behaviour is a reportable feature of the analysis, not
an implementation detail. If it fires on any primary model with real data, the manuscript's methods
must state which covariates were removed from which models and why. The guard's output is written to
the console and retained on the results object precisely so that this reporting is straightforward.
A useful secondary reading is diagnostic: extensive firing on a configuration is itself evidence that
the configuration is underpowered for the adjustment set, and that its estimates should be
interpreted as exploratory.

%======================================================================
\needspace{14\baselineskip}
\section{Secondary Analysis: Restricted Cubic Splines}
%======================================================================
The primary analysis divides each exposure into quartiles. That is robust,
makes no functional-form assumption, and matches the published template --- but
it discards within-quartile variation, imposes step changes at cut-points that
carry no biological meaning, and cannot recover the \emph{shape} of a
dose--response relationship. A U-shaped association, in which both very short
and very long sleep carry elevated odds, is precisely the pattern a quartile
model is least able to express: Q1 and Q4 both appear elevated relative to a
mid-range reference, but the model cannot say where the minimum lies or whether
the rise at either end is real.

This section adds a spline analysis alongside the quartile models. It replaces
nothing: both analyses are fitted on identical participants with an identical
adjustment set, so any difference between them is attributable to the exposure
parameterisation and to nothing else.

\subsection{Methodological precedent} The procedure follows Master et al.,
\emph{Nature Medicine} 2022;28:2301--2308,
``Association of step counts over time with the risk of chronic disease in the
All of Us Research Program'' (doi:10.1038/s41591-022-02012-w). That study is the
closest available precedent: the same cohort resource, Fitbit-derived exposures,
EHR-derived outcomes, and Frank Harrell as senior methodologist. It also reports
acid reflux among its outcomes, so the exposure--outcome pair examined here is
one it examined directly.

\subsection{Procedure} For each continuous exposure:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Fit restricted cubic splines with 3, 4 and 5 knots in separate logistic
models, each adjusted for the full covariate set.
\item Select the knot count with the lowest AIC. All three candidates are fitted
on the same rows, so the comparison is valid.
\item Test the exposure with Harrell's Wald ``chunk test'', reported as two
separate quantities:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{$p$-overall} --- jointly, are \emph{all} spline terms zero? This
asks whether the exposure is associated with the outcome at all.
\item \textbf{$p$-non-linearity} --- jointly, are the \emph{non-linear} terms
zero? This asks whether that association departs from a straight line on the
log-odds scale.
\end{itemize}
\item Report the effect as the odds ratio comparing the 75th to the 25th
percentile of the exposure, matching the contrast Master et al.\ report.
\item Plot the fitted curve with a 95\% confidence band across the observed
range, referenced to the median.
\end{enumerate}

Knots sit at Harrell's recommended quantiles (\emph{Regression Modeling
Strategies}, Table 2.3), which are the \code{rms::rcs()} defaults:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Knots} & \textbf{Quantiles} \\
\midrule
3 & 10, 50, 90 \\
4 & 5, 35, 65, 95 \\
5 & 5, 27.5, 50, 72.5, 95 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{The spline basis} With knots $t_1 < \cdots < t_k$, the basis is the
truncated power form of \emph{Regression Modeling Strategies} eq.~2.25: the
first column is $x$ itself and, for $j = 1, \ldots, k-2$,
\[
X_{j+1} = \frac{(x-t_j)_+^3
 - (x-t_{k-1})_+^3\,\frac{t_k-t_j}{t_k-t_{k-1}}
 + (x-t_k)_+^3\,\frac{t_{k-1}-t_j}{t_k-t_{k-1}}}{(t_k-t_1)^2}.
\]
The linear term occupying column one is what makes the two chunk tests a clean
partition: dropping columns $2,\ldots,k-1$ leaves a model linear in $x$, and
dropping all of them removes $x$ entirely. The basis is implemented directly
rather than through \code{rms}, so the pipeline carries no additional dependency,
and it is verified on load against \code{splines::ns()} --- the two bases span
the same function space, so a model fitted with either must produce identical
fitted values. The run aborts if that check fails.

\subsection{Odds ratio curves} For a model
$\operatorname{logit}\Pr(Y=1) = \beta_0 + \mathbf{B}(x)^\top\boldsymbol\beta + \mathbf{Z}^\top\boldsymbol\gamma$,
the odds ratio at $x$ relative to a reference $x_0$ is
\[
\mathrm{OR}(x \,\vert\, x_0) = \exp\!\big[(\mathbf{B}(x)-\mathbf{B}(x_0))^\top \boldsymbol\beta\big],
\qquad
\widehat{\mathrm{Var}} = \mathbf{d}^\top \mathbf{V}_{\boldsymbol\beta}\, \mathbf{d},
\quad \mathbf{d} = \mathbf{B}(x)-\mathbf{B}(x_0).
\]
Every covariate is held fixed and cancels exactly, so only the spline block of
the coefficient vector and its covariance sub-matrix are required. The reference
and both contrast percentiles are placed on the evaluation grid, so the plotted
curve passes exactly through $\mathrm{OR}=1$ at the reference rather than merely
near it.

\subsection{Two deliberate departures from Master et al.} Both follow from this
study's design and are stated here so that neither is mistaken for an oversight.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Logistic regression, not Cox.} This study is cross-sectional.
There is no follow-up time and no incident-event structure, so a proportional
hazards model is not defined; odds ratios replace hazard ratios. Master et al.\
likewise used logistic regression for their own cross-sectional screening step
and reserved Cox models for the longitudinal analysis their repeated-measures
design supported.
\item \textbf{Complete-case, not multiple imputation.} The paper pooled results
over five \code{aregImpute} datasets. Complete-case analysis is used here so
that the spline and quartile models are fitted on exactly the same rows. Adding
imputation to one arm only would confound a difference in \emph{shape} with a
difference in \emph{missing-data handling}, which is the one comparison this
section exists to make. Per-model missingness is reported so the cost is visible,
and multiple imputation across both analyses remains available as a sensitivity
analysis.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Stability safeguards} A spline model can converge and still be
numerically meaningless. With few cases, cubic terms can yield an odds ratio of
$10^5$ with a confidence interval spanning twenty orders of magnitude, and --- as
observed during validation --- such a model can carry an apparently significant
$p$-value. Three guards prevent that reaching a table:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{8.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Guard} & \textbf{Action} \\
\midrule
CI ratio $>1000$ & Estimate suppressed, row marked \textsc{unstable} \\
$\lvert \log \mathrm{OR}\rvert > \log 50$ & Estimate suppressed, row marked \textsc{unstable} \\
Events per parameter $<10$ & Estimate retained, flagged as provisional \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent Suppressed rows remain in the CSV with the reason recorded in
\code{unstable\_why}, so nothing is silently discarded, but they are excluded
from the shape classification and displayed as \code{--}. The events-per-variable
threshold follows Peduzzi et al.\ (1996); it matters more here than in the
quartile analysis because a 5-knot spline spends four degrees of freedom on the
exposure alone.

\subsection{Validation} The implementation is verified against data with known
structure before use: a simulated U-shape must be detected as non-linear with
its minimum recovered in the right place; a simulated linear trend must be
detected as an association but \emph{not} as non-linear; a null exposure must
trigger neither test; and the 75th-versus-25th-percentile odds ratio must agree
with an independently fitted linear-logit model. All four checks, together with
the basis identity against \code{splines::ns()}, pass.

\subsection{Outputs} Written to \code{manuscript\_output\_splines/}, separate
from the quartile outputs:

\begin{itemize}
\item \code{<cohort>\_spline\_summary.csv} --- one row per outcome $\times$
exposure: selected knot count, knot locations, AIC, OR (75th vs 25th) with CI,
both $\chi^2$ statistics with degrees of freedom, both raw and multiplicity-
adjusted $p$-values, events per parameter, and a \code{shape} column reading
\emph{linear}, \emph{non-linear}, \emph{no association} or \textsc{unstable}.
\item \code{<cohort>\_<outcome>\_<exposure>\_spline.png} --- the fitted curve
with its confidence band, knot locations, reference line, and the exposure
distribution beneath, so that a wide interval in a sparse region is not misread
as a finding.
\item \code{<cohort>\_<outcome>\_<exposure>\_curve.csv} --- the plotted
coordinates, so figures can be restyled for submission without refitting.
\end{itemize}

Multiplicity is handled exactly as in the primary analysis: Bonferroni within
outcome, across exposures, governed by the same \code{GERD\_P\_ADJUST} setting.

\subsection{Interpretation} The two analyses answer different questions and
should be read together. The quartile models give the manuscript's headline
effect estimates in the familiar form; the spline models establish whether the
quartile pattern reflects a genuine monotone gradient, a threshold, or a
U-shape, and where any inflection lies. Where $p$-non-linearity is not
significant, the linear-in-log-odds summary is adequate and the quartile
estimates can be reported without qualification. Where it \emph{is} significant,
the quartile table understates the relationship and the spline figure should
carry the interpretation.

%======================================================================
\needspace{12\baselineskip}
\section{Quartile versus Spline: Did the Secondary Analysis Change Anything?}
%======================================================================
Two analyses now estimate the same associations, which raises an obvious
question that should not require reading two sets of tables side by side to
answer. \code{GERD Quartile vs Spline Comparison R9.R} answers it in one file
per cohort, written at the end of the spline run.

\subsection{Making the comparison fair} Three differences would make a naive
side-by-side misleading. All three are eliminated by \emph{refitting} every
model inside the comparison rather than reading the two analyses' output files.

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{The contrasts differ.} The quartile model reports Q4 versus Q1;
the spline reports the 75th versus the 25th percentile. Q4 versus Q1 contrasts
the middles of the top and bottom quarters, which lie further apart than
$P_{75}$ and $P_{25}$, so comparing the two directly would show an effect-size
difference that is an artefact of the contrast rather than of the method. The
comparison therefore evaluates the spline at the \emph{median of Q1} and the
\emph{median of Q4}, so both columns answer the same question. The
$P_{75}$-versus-$P_{25}$ contrast is retained in its own columns.
\item \textbf{The row sets differ.} The spline analysis trims to the
1st--99th percentile; the quartile analysis does not. AIC and interval widths
are not comparable across different rows, so the trim is applied to both.
\item \textbf{The covariate sets can differ.} The sparsity guard may drop
different covariates in the two fits. Here it runs once and the surviving set is
used for all three models.
\end{enumerate}

\noindent With those controlled, any remaining difference is attributable to the
exposure parameterisation, which is the only thing under test.

\subsection{What each row reports} Odds ratios from both methods on the matched
contrast; $\Delta$AIC (spline minus quartile, negative favouring the spline);
the ratio of confidence-interval widths on the log-odds scale, below one meaning
the spline is more precise; the non-linearity $p$-value; and a plain-language
\code{verdict}:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{8.2cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Verdict} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\midrule
\textsc{spline adds}: non-linear & The relationship is not log-linear. The quartile table understates it; the spline figure should carry the interpretation. \\
\textsc{spline adds}: association only the spline detects & The quartile model missed it. \\
disagree & The two methods differ on significance. Investigate before reporting either. \\
agree; spline more precise & Same conclusion, narrower interval. \\
agree & Same conclusion. Report quartiles as primary; cite the spline as confirming log-linearity. \\
unstable & Too few cases. Suppressed on whichever side is affected; not reportable. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Why this is worth having even when the answer is ``no''} A verdict of
\emph{agree} across the board is a substantive result, not a null one: it
establishes that the quartile estimates are not an artefact of where the
cut-points fell, and it forecloses the reviewer question ``did you check the
functional form?''. One methods sentence then covers it.

\subsection{The case this exists to catch} During validation, a simulated
symmetric U-shape produced a quartile odds ratio of $1.00$ (0.91--1.11) --- an
exactly null result --- because Q1 and Q4 sit at comparable risk when the
minimum lies in the middle, so the contrast between them cancels. The spline
recovered the same relationship with $p_{\text{non-linearity}} < 10^{-4}$ and
$\Delta\mathrm{AIC} = -2049$. A quartile analysis alone would have reported no
association where a strong one exists. This is not a hypothetical failure mode:
it is the specific reason a sleep exposure warrants the check, since both short
and long sleep are plausibly associated with reflux.

\subsection{Stability applies to both sides}
The suppression rules of the spline section
are applied to the quartile estimate as well as the spline. A quartile model is
no less capable of degeneracy --- validation produced one with an odds ratio of
$4.9 \times 10^9$ --- and a table that suppressed only the spline would imply
the other side was trustworthy when it was not.

%======================================================================
\section{Data Engineering, Quality Control, and Statistical Considerations}
%======================================================================

\subsection{One-row-per-participant guarantee}
Every derived covariate table is reduced to one row per \code{person_id} before joining, and the
diagnostics assert no duplicate identifiers in the modeling frame. Eligibility is defined once (an
\code{inner_join} to the filtered exposure summary), and covariates are attached by \code{left_join} so
they never expand the row count.

\subsection{Missing data}
Binary EHR indicators (conditions, medications, PUD, IBS, and the outcomes) are coalesced from
\code{NA} to \code{FALSE}: in EHR phenotyping the absence of a qualifying record means the participant
does not meet the phenotype. Charlson score is coalesced to $0$ when no components precede the exposure
window. Survey/measurement covariates (education, income, alcohol, BMI) are \emph{not} imputed; an
explicit ``Unknown/Missing'' level is carried through and reported, so differential missingness by
outcome is visible. Each adjusted model uses complete cases over its own variable set, so the effective
$N$ is reported per model and can differ across exposures.

\subsection{Multiple comparisons}
With fifteen exposures (each with several quartile contrasts) against two outcomes across three exposure
sets, multiplicity is substantial. The analysis applies the Bonferroni correction across the models
(matching the antecedent IBS paper; \code{GERD_P_ADJUST} selects FDR instead), and the primary multivariable emphasis is on effect sizes
(aOR) and confidence intervals under a fixed, pre-specified adjustment set rather than on dichotomous
significance.

\subsection{Confounding structure}
Each covariate blocks a plausible backdoor path: adiposity (BMI) raises intra-abdominal pressure and
also shortens/fragments sleep and reduces activity; age, sex, race, ethnicity, and socio-economic
status pattern both behavior and health-care coding; smoking and alcohol relax the lower esophageal
sphincter and degrade sleep; depression and anxiety alter sleep and amplify symptom reporting; IBS and
PUD co-travel with GERD; the Charlson index captures residual multimorbidity; and sleep-relevant
medications affect measured sleep. Mediators of the exposure$\rightarrow$GERD path (acid-suppression
therapy) are excluded from the primary adjustment set to avoid over-adjustment and used only to define
the treated-GERD sensitivity outcome.

\subsection{Sample size and precision}
No fixed sample size is imposed; precision is governed by the number of \emph{cases}. Oesophagitis,
being the erosive subset, is rarer than broad GERD, and the treated-GERD sensitivity (a further subset)
is the most precision-limited. The combined intersection cohort is smaller than either single-exposure
cohort. Sparse covariate$\times$outcome cells are exactly what the separation diagnostic is designed to
catch, and backward-AIC can drop unstable terms while the exposure is retained.

%======================================================================
\section{Assumed Causal Structure}
%======================================================================
The adjustment set is not chosen empirically (for example, by stepwise inclusion of ``significant''
covariates) but from an explicit, if informal, causal diagram. Stating the assumed structure makes the
identification strategy auditable and clarifies which variables must be adjusted for and which must not.

\subsection{Paths the design intends to block}
The exposures (sleep, activity) and the outcomes (GERD, oesophagitis) share several common causes that
open backdoor paths and would bias an unadjusted association:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \textbf{Adiposity} $\rightarrow$ both worse sleep/less activity and more reflux (pressure
  gradient). Adjusted via median BMI.
  \item \textbf{Age, sex, race, ethnicity, socioeconomic status} $\rightarrow$ both behavior and
  health-care coding. Adjusted via the demographic and SES covariates.
  \item \textbf{Psychiatric comorbidity} (depression, anxiety) $\rightarrow$ both sleep and
  symptom reporting. Adjusted directly.
  \item \textbf{General multimorbidity and GI comorbidity} (Charlson, PUD, IBS) $\rightarrow$ both
  activity/sleep and reflux coding. Adjusted directly.
  \item \textbf{Health-care surveillance intensity} $\rightarrow$ both wearable adoption and diagnosis
  probability. Partially blocked by SES and comorbidity adjustment.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Paths the design intends \emph{not} to block}
Two categories are deliberately excluded from the primary adjustment set:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \textbf{Mediators} of the exposure$\rightarrow$outcome effect. Acid-suppression therapy plausibly
  lies \emph{downstream} of reflux; conditioning on it would be over-adjustment and could induce
  collider bias. It is therefore used only to \emph{define} the treated-GERD sensitivity outcome, never
  as a covariate.
  \item \textbf{Colliders.} No variable that is a common \emph{effect} of exposure and outcome is
  conditioned on, since doing so would open a non-causal path.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The reverse-causation edge}
The one edge the covariate set cannot address is the direct outcome$\rightarrow$exposure arrow: nocturnal
reflux fragments sleep. Adjustment cannot remove a reverse-causal effect; only design can. The
post-Fitbit temporal outcome definition addresses it by requiring the outcome's EHR appearance to follow
the exposure-measurement window, and its concordance with the primary estimate is reported as the key
robustness check.

%======================================================================
\section{Sensitivity Analyses}
\label{sec:sensitivity}
%======================================================================
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=3pt]
  \item \textbf{Distinct-date GERD} (\code{has_gerd_any_dates}) --- the stricter ``$\geq 2$ distinct dates''
  phenotype, testing robustness to same-day code inflation.
  \item \textbf{Post-Fitbit temporal GERD} (\code{has_post_fitbit_gerd}) --- restricts cases to those
  whose first GERD code appears $\geq 180$ days \emph{after} the first Fitbit date, so the measured
  exposure precedes the outcome's EHR appearance. This is the strongest available guard against reverse
  causation, which is a genuine concern because nocturnal reflux fragments sleep.
  \item \textbf{Sleep$\times$activity overlap} --- repeats analyses on the concurrently well-measured
  subset.
  \item \textbf{Medication-treated (``severe'') GERD} (\code{severe_gerd_binary}) --- an optional
  outcome flagging participants who both meet the GERD phenotype \emph{and} have $\geq 2$ PPI/$H_2$RA
  prescription dates beginning at least one day after their first GERD diagnosis, an EHR proxy for
  clinically managed disease. It mirrors the IBS study's ``severe IBS'' construction but substitutes
  acid-suppression pharmacotherapy.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{The pharmacologics pull}
The treated-GERD analysis depends on \code{GERD Pharmacologics Data Upload.R}, which retrieves drug
exposures whose ancestor concept is a PPI (\code{21600095}) or an $H_2$RA (\code{21600096}) using the
\code{concept_ancestor} double-join (the same structure as the IBS drug pull), restricted to
Fitbit-sharers. The whole treated-GERD section is guarded: it runs only if \code{R9_gerd_drug_df.rds}
exists, so the primary pipeline executes cleanly whether or not the optional pull has been performed.

%======================================================================
\section{Relationship to the IBS and Constipation Templates}
%======================================================================
The suite mirrors the project's IBS sleep study, constipation activity study, and constipation outcome
upload, but global find-and-replace was insufficient; whole blocks were removed, added, or corrected.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{2.1cm}p{11.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Action} & \textbf{Detail} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Swapped} & \code{has_ibs} $\rightarrow$ \code{has_gerd_any} plus \code{has_esophagitis}; outcome
labels; output filenames $\rightarrow$ \code{R9_final_analysis_*_gerd_df.rds}. \\
\textbf{Swapped source} & IBS rows from \code{dataset_32338507_condition_df} $\rightarrow$ the dedicated
\code{R9_gerd_outcome} pull. \\
\textbf{Removed} & All IBS-subtype logic (subtype flags, most-frequent/tie-break assignment,
mutually-exclusive \code{has_ibs_c/d/m/general}, multinomial models); the standalone severe-IBS block
was replaced by the optional, guarded treated-GERD section. \\
\textbf{Added} & \code{has_esophagitis} as a second outcome; \code{has_pud}, \code{has_sleep_apnea}, \code{on_sleep_med} and \code{on_narcotic}
covariates; the activity and combined analyses; the mutually-adjusted sleep$+$activity models; and the
shared modeling engine. \\
\textbf{Fixed} & Consistent \code{R9_*} inputs throughout; one canonical
\code{first_fitbit_sleep_date_df} / \code{first_fitbit_date_df} and \code{sleep_clean_df}, resolving the
naming mismatches present in the IBS script. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{IBS/constipation $\rightarrow$ GERD/oesophagitis transformation.}
\end{table}

\noindent Every GERD-specific artifact is \code{R9_}-prefixed or \code{_gerd}-suffixed, so running the
GERD pipeline never overwrites an IBS or constipation artifact even though the underlying
sleep-cleaning and activity-summary steps are numerically identical.

%======================================================================
\section{Reproducibility and Execution}
%======================================================================
\subsection{Execution order}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item In the workspace, verify the GERD/oesophagitis seed concepts in the Cohort Builder, then run
  \code{GERD Outcome Data Upload R9.R} $\rightarrow$ \code{R9_gerd_outcome.rds}.
  \item Ensure \code{GERD Analysis Helpers R9.R} is in the working directory (each analysis file
  \code{source()}s it).
  \item Run any of \code{Sleep and GERD Analysis File R9.R}, \code{Activity and GERD Analysis File
  R9.R}, and \code{Activity Sleep and GERD Analysis File R9.R} --- each produces both outcomes.
  \item (Optional) Run \code{GERD Pharmacologics Data Upload.R} $\rightarrow$ \code{R9_gerd_drug_df.rds},
  then re-run the sleep analysis to activate the treated-GERD section.
\end{enumerate}
\subsection{Software environment}
The pipeline uses \code{tidyverse}, \code{gtsummary}, \code{broom}, \code{lubridate}, \code{MASS},
\code{car}, \code{pROC}, \code{forcats}, and \code{rlang}, plus \code{bigrquery} for the pulls. The
helper installs missing packages on first load. Because all modeling lives in the helper, a change
there propagates to all six configurations at once.

%======================================================================
\section{Verification and Defects Found}
\label{sec:verify}
%======================================================================
Because the analysis cannot be executed against real participant data outside the secure workspace,
verification was designed to establish as much as possible without it. Three stages were used.

\subsection{Stage 1 --- schema validation}
Every dataset the pipeline touches was opened and its columns and types confirmed directly, rather
than inferred from the antecedent scripts. This covered the raw Fitbit, EHR, survey and person tables
and all pre-built covariate objects, and produced Tables~\ref{tab:rawschema} and~\ref{tab:prebuilt}.
Several assumptions carried over from the antecedent code proved incorrect at this stage, notably the
duplicated \code{n_valid_days} column and the suffixed \code{answer_concept_id} columns.

\subsection{Stage 2 --- schema-exact synthetic data}
Synthetic datasets were then constructed whose column names and types were checked field by field
against the real schema definitions, with construction aborting on any mismatch. The synthetic data
deliberately reproduced the hazards rather than an idealised structure:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item the duplicated \code{n_valid_days} column across two joined objects;
  \item the \code{answer_concept_id.x}/\code{.y} pair in the alcohol object;
  \item en-dashed factor levels in \code{age_cat} and \code{cci_cat};
  \item character-typed \code{date_of_birth} and \code{condition_start_datetime};
  \item an ultra-rare \code{has_pud} (20 of 3,000, mirroring the real 137) to exercise the guard;
  \item GERD records spanning both pre- and post-Fitbit windows, so that the two case definitions
  would genuinely diverge.
\end{itemize}
The outcome builder was additionally checked against four hand-constructed participants whose
expected classification under each rule was known in advance: three late codes (post-Fitbit case), two
early codes (case under the ``ever'' rule only), a single code (case under neither), and two late
oesophagitis codes (case under both phenotypes).

\subsection{Stage 3 --- end-to-end execution}
All three drivers were then run to completion against that data. The final status was \textbf{exit
code 0 for all three}, producing 50 output files with zero errors and zero warnings. The mutually
adjusted pair models of configuration C5/C6 were additionally exercised in isolation.

\subsection{Defects found and fixed}
\label{sec:defects}
Five defects surfaced during schema alignment and execution. Each would have either aborted the run or,
more insidiously, produced plausible-looking but wrong results.

\begin{table}[h]\centering\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.6cm}p{4.4cm}p{5.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Defect} & \textbf{Consequence} & \textbf{Fix} \\
\midrule
\code{library(MASS)} masked \code{dplyr::select()} & Immediate failure of the first \code{select()}
call in the pipeline; would have blocked the run entirely & MASS, car and pROC are referenced with
\code{::} and never attached; tidyverse packages attach with dplyr last \\
\addlinespace
Duplicated \code{n_valid_days} & Joining the wear-hours and steps summaries produced
\code{n_valid_days.x/.y}; the descriptive table's coverage row would have failed or silently used the
wrong column & The wear-hours object is slimmed to two columns before joining \\
\addlinespace
Suffixed \code{answer_concept_id} & Contaminated the covariate bundle and collided with the education
and income extracts & Only \code{alcohol_likert_final} is taken \\
\addlinespace
Free-text socio-economic mapping & The \code{*_response} strings did not match the antecedent script's
\code{case_when}; \emph{every} participant would have collapsed to ``Missing'', silently destroying
two covariates & Categories mapped from \code{answer_concept_id}, with text matching as fallback \\
\addlinespace
En-dashed factor levels & \code{cci_cat} levels failed to match, producing 50+ level-mismatch warnings
and risking silent \code{NA}s & Dash style normalised before factor construction \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Defects identified by schema alignment and end-to-end execution.}
\label{tab:defects}
\end{table}

\noindent The fourth is the most instructive. It produces no error, no warning, and a complete set of
output tables; the only symptom is that education and income appear entirely uninformative. The
antecedent script's own comments record having encountered exactly this and worked around it by
hand. Mapping from concept identifiers removes the failure mode rather than the symptom.

\subsection{What this verification does and does not establish}
It establishes that the code executes correctly against the real \emph{schema}, that the join hazards
are handled, that the outcome definitions classify participants as intended, that the separation guard
engages when it should, and that the emitted tables and figures match the target format.

It cannot establish anything about the real data's missingness patterns, case counts, or covariate
distributions, because those are not observable outside the workspace. Two console outputs should
therefore be checked on the first production run. The eligible sleep cohort should print
$N = 19{,}995$; a different value means a different \code{valid_population_sleep} object was picked up
and the cohort no longer matches the published paper. And \code{build_gerd_outcomes()} prints case
counts under both definitions; if the post-Fitbit count is very small relative to the ``ever'' count,
that is a substantive finding about GERD ascertainment timing in this cohort and may argue for making
the ``ever'' rule primary.

\subsection{Stage 4 --- defects found in the first full run}
\label{sec:defects-run}
Executing the pipeline against real data surfaced three defects that synthetic testing could not,
because each produces well-formed output. All three affect the activity arm only.

\paragraph{D1. \code{avg_very_active_min} duplicates \code{avg_fairly_active_min}.}
In the build, both are assigned \code{mean(min_cardio)}:
\begin{quote}\ttfamily\footnotesize
avg\_fairly\_active\_min = mean(min\_cardio, na.rm = TRUE),\\
avg\_very\_active\_min~~ = mean(min\_cardio, na.rm = TRUE),
\end{quote}
They are one variable under two names, which is why their odds ratios agree to the last digit in
every configuration. Consequences: the activity arm reports seven exposures but measures six; two
Bonferroni tests are spent on the same quantity, making the correction slightly conservative; and
any reader comparing the two rows would reasonably conclude the analysis is broken. The intended
source for ``very active'' is the Fitbit \textbf{Peak} zone --- see D2.

\paragraph{D2. The Peak heart-rate zone is never captured.}
\code{hr_daily} sums \code{minute_in_zone} for \code{"cardio"}, \code{"fat burn"} and
\code{"out of range"} only. Fitbit's fourth zone, \textbf{Peak}, is silently dropped, so the
highest-intensity minutes are absent from every activity exposure, including
\code{avg_total_active_min}. This is a floor effect on the most physiologically interesting end of
the distribution.

\paragraph{D3. Wear time is not adjusted for.}
\code{GERD_ADJ_COVARS_BASE} contains no wear-time or coverage term, yet the activity exposures are
absolute minutes per day whose magnitude depends on how many hours the device was worn. The
antecedent published analysis treats this as first-order --- its file is named
\textit{IBS and Activity (Controlling for Wear Time).R}. \code{avg_daily_wear_hours} is already
built and saved by the pipeline (\code{R9_avg_wear_hours_df.rds}); it is simply not used. Until it
is, the activity estimates in Section~\ref{sec:activity-anomaly} confound activity with adherence.

\paragraph{D4. \code{on_narcotic} is TRUE for 100\% of every cohort.}
The opioid-analgesic covariate is positive for every participant in all six configurations
(e.g.\ 13{,}215/13{,}215 and 725/725 in the sleep cohort). A constant carries no information, so
the covariate adjusts for nothing. It is set from the per-class medication export
(\code{GB_MED_DIRS[["on_narcotic"]]}) by \code{meds[[k]] <- meds\$person_id \%in\% ids}, so a
Narcotics folder containing every participant rather than only those with an exposure would
produce exactly this. The export contents should be checked before the flag is used or the
expert-requested opioid adjustment is claimed.

\paragraph{D5. \code{has_pud} is FALSE for 100\% of every cohort.}
Peptic ulcer disease is absent from all 35{,}670 participants across the three cohorts. A
population prevalence of exactly zero is not credible, so concept \code{4027663} is either wrong,
or not being expanded to its descendants, or absent from this export. The covariate is therefore
inert, and it is also one of the four terms in the comorbidity index (D6).

\paragraph{D6. The comorbidity index cannot reach its top category.}
\code{cci_score} is the sum of four binary flags (heart failure, COPD, diabetes, peptic ulcer), so
its maximum possible value is 4 --- yet \code{cci_cat} defines a \code{"5+"} level, which is
unreachable by construction. With \code{has_pud} always FALSE (D5) the effective maximum is 3.
Observed data bear this out: the \code{"3-4"} and \code{"5+"} categories are $0\%$ in all six
configurations. The report already flags this index as not a validated Charlson score; these two
levels should simply be removed.

\paragraph{D7. ``Maximum daily heart rate'' is the constant 220 for every participant.}
\code{max_heart_rate} in \code{heartRateSummary} is the \emph{upper boundary of each heart-rate
zone}, not an observed heart rate. Taking \code{max()} across a person's zones therefore returns
the ceiling of the top zone --- 220 --- for everyone. The quartile-cutoff output confirms it: min,
p25, median, p75 and max are all exactly 220.

The damaging part is what happened next. \code{ntile()} on a constant breaks ties by row position,
so four arbitrary ``quartiles'' were manufactured and modelled. Table~2 reports
\emph{Maximum daily heart rate quartiles} with $p < 0.001$, and the multivariable table carries
four rows of odds ratios and confidence intervals for it. None survives Bonferroni correction
($p_{\text{adj}} = 1$ throughout), so the headline findings are not contaminated --- but a
descriptive $p < 0.001$ for a variable with no variance would badly mislead a reader, and the
exposure must be removed rather than reported as null. The spline pipeline already skips it.

\paragraph{D8. ``Sedentary minutes'' is a wear-time measure, not a behaviour.}
\code{avg_sedentary_min} is the mean of the Out-of-Range heart-rate zone. Its observed median is
1{,}382 minutes per day --- 23 hours --- with a p75 of 1{,}418 and a maximum of exactly 1{,}440.
A ``sedentary'' exposure that occupies essentially the entire day is measuring how long the device
recorded a heart rate, not how much the participant sat. This is the same confound as D3 seen from
the other side, and it explains the implausibly protective aOR of $0.23$ in
Section~\ref{sec:activity-anomaly}: the highest ``sedentary'' quartile is largely the
longest-wearing quartile. For scale, the active zones in the same export have medians of 46
(lightly), 3.5 (fairly/very) and 52 (total) minutes per day.

\paragraph{Recommended sequence before these results are circulated.}
Drop the \code{max_hr} exposure (D7) and the unreachable comorbidity levels (D6). Investigate the
Narcotics export (D4) and concept \code{4027663} (D5); until both are resolved, state plainly that
neither opioid use nor peptic ulcer disease is actually adjusted for, contrary to the protocol.
Fix D1 and D2, replace or rename the sedentary exposure (D8), and add wear time --- and, per
Section~\ref{sec:verify}, a coverage term --- to the activity adjustment set. Then re-run
configurations 2, 3, 5 and 6.

The sleep results (Configurations 1 and 4) are unaffected by D1, D2, D3, D7 and D8, which are
activity-arm defects. They are touched by D4, D5 and D6 only in that three intended covariates
turn out to be inert, so the sleep models are adjusted for less than the protocol states. Given
the effect sizes involved --- restless minutes at aOR 3.41 --- that is unlikely to overturn the
direction, but it must be disclosed rather than left implicit. The selection issue described under
Table~1 applies to the sleep arm in full.

reference throughout. Every number below is reproduced mechanically from the output files.
Defects uncovered by this run are catalogued in Section~\ref{sec:defects-run} and referenced from
the affected results.

\noindent GERD prevalence is $5.2\%$ in the sleep cohort and $4.7\%$ in the activity cohort;
oesophagitis is $1.2\%$ and $1.1\%$. Complete-case attrition is close to $11\%$ in every cohort.
The cohorts share no denominator with the IBS paper: they are built entirely from the new export.

\subsection{Cohort characteristics, and the warning they carry}
\label{sec:res-t1s}
The baseline table is not neutral background here --- it is the clearest evidence that the case
group is selected rather than representative. GERD cases are younger (median 51 vs 56), more often
female, higher-income, less likely to smoke, and carry \emph{less} depression, anxiety and diabetes
than non-cases. Every one of those runs opposite to the direction the GERD literature would
predict.

Read alongside the coverage gap --- cases contribute 1{,}166 [556, 1{,}920] valid nights against
569 [363, 1{,}223] for non-cases --- and the post-Fitbit case rule, the most economical explanation
is that becoming a case in this design requires early Fitbit adoption and sustained wear, which
selects a younger, wealthier, healthier and more engaged subgroup. This is a property of the case
definition, not a discovery about GERD, and it applies to all six configurations. Obstructive sleep
apnoea, the covariate the GERD expert specifically requested, is identically distributed between
the groups ($19\%$ vs $19\%$, $p=0.9$), so it is not driving the sleep findings.

Two rows of Table~\ref{tab:res-t1} are degenerate and are discussed as defects D4 and D5: every
participant is flagged as taking an opioid analgesic, and no participant has peptic ulcer disease.

\subsection{Univariate associations of the covariates}
\label{sec:res-cov}
The \code{supp_table1_univariate.csv} files report crude odds ratios for every covariate as well as
every exposure. These rows are worth reading in full, because they quantify the selection pattern
described in Section~\ref{sec:res-t1s} rather than merely asserting it. In the sleep cohort:
participants aged 65 and over have $0.38$ times the odds of being a GERD case as those under 30
($0.29$--$0.50$); those earning \$150k or more have $1.89$ times the odds of the lowest income band
($1.51$--$2.36$); and depression ($0.71$), anxiety ($0.56$) and the comorbidity index ($0.61$ for
1--2 conditions) are all \emph{protective}.

No plausible biology makes affluence a risk factor for reflux and anxiety a protective one. These
are the fingerprints of a case definition that requires early device adoption and sustained wear.
The same pattern holds in the activity and combined cohorts.

\subsection{Sleep exposures and GERD (Configuration 1)}
\label{sec:res-sleep}
Five of the eight sleep exposures survive Bonferroni correction, and the pattern is the study's
central finding: the signal lies in sleep \emph{architecture and fragmentation}, not sleep
\emph{quantity}. Minutes asleep, minutes in bed and sleep efficiency are flatly null across all
four quartiles, while restless minutes and all three sleep stages are strongly and monotonically
associated. Unadjusted and adjusted estimates are close throughout --- the measured demographic,
behavioural and comorbid covariates explain little of the association.

\subsection{Sleep exposures and oesophagitis (Configuration 4)}
With 164 cases the same directions hold with wider intervals, and minutes asleep, minutes in
bed and sleep efficiency remain null. The stricter phenotype corroborates rather than
contradicts the broad one, which is the most reassuring internal check the study contains:
oesophagitis is endoscopically verified and far less susceptible to coding noise than a GERD
symptom code.
