\subsection{Activity exposures --- direction contrary to hypothesis}
\label{sec:activity-anomaly}
The activity results run opposite to the pre-specified hypothesis and are large: more active
minutes predict \emph{higher} GERD odds and more sedentary time \emph{lower} odds.
\textbf{These estimates must not be interpreted biologically as they stand.} Five of the eight
defects in Section~\ref{sec:defects-run} bear directly on the activity tables.

The decisive internal evidence is a contrast within the table itself. \emph{Daily steps} is the one
activity exposure that is a direct accelerometer count rather than a heart-rate-zone derivative,
and it is the one exposure that does \emph{not} survive correction (Q4 aOR $1.34$,
$p_{\text{adj}}=0.24$), attenuating from its unadjusted $1.58$. Every zone-derived exposure --- all
of which scale with how long the device was worn --- carries a large ``effect''. A biological
mechanism that raised reflux risk with activity would not spare the only clean activity measure.

\subsection{Combined cohort (Configurations 3 and 6)}
\label{sec:res-comb}
The combined cohort reproduces both arms with almost unchanged estimates, fitting 15 models per
outcome against 8 and 7 in the single-modality cohorts. Full results are in
Tables~\ref{tab:res-comb-gerd} and~\ref{tab:res-comb-eso}.

\subsection{Mutually adjusted sleep\texorpdfstring{$\times$}{x}activity pairs}
\label{sec:res-mutual}
Four pre-specified pairs were fitted with a sleep term and an activity term in the same model. This
is the most informative robustness check the run contains, because it asks whether the sleep
associations are the activity artefact in another guise. They are not: deep sleep retains
aOR $0.65$ ($0.52$--$0.82$) with total active minutes in the model, and REM retains $0.64$
($0.51$--$0.82$) alongside sedentary minutes, while the two null sleep exposures stay null.

\subsection{Restricted cubic splines}
\label{sec:res-spline}
Splines were fitted for every continuous exposure with knot count chosen by AIC. The constant
max-heart-rate exposure was correctly skipped, giving 12 activity rows rather than 14. In the sleep
cohort the exposures split cleanly into two non-linear (restless, light sleep), three linear
(awake, deep, REM) and three null; the activity cohort is uniformly non-linear, which is what a
wear-time gradient would produce rather than a dose--response. Under splines, daily steps reaches
nominal significance (OR $1.20$ per P25$\to$P75, $p_{\text{adj}}=0.041$) where the quartile model
did not --- the only place the two methods materially disagree.

For oesophagitis, events per variable fall to $5.5$--$7.1$ and every spline row is flagged
\code{UNSTABLE}. Those fits are directional only and must not be quoted with their intervals.

\subsection{Quartile versus spline}
\label{sec:res-qvs}
The two methods never contradict each other on direction across all 56 pairs. Confidence intervals
from the spline are $0.76$--$1.12\times$ the quartile width, so the spline is generally the more
precise estimator without being materially wider anywhere.

\subsection{Shape of the fitted curves}
\label{sec:res-curves}
The 56 curve files give the fitted odds ratio across 203 points spanning each observed exposure
range. Thirty-nine of the 56 have an interior turning point rather than a monotone trend. For the
two clearly non-linear sleep relationships the shape is interpretable:

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Restless minutes} rise steeply from the reference to a peak odds ratio of $2.40$ at
about 18 minutes per night, then flatten. Risk is concentrated in the transition from almost no
restlessness to a moderate amount; further restlessness adds little.
\item \textbf{Light sleep} reaches its highest odds ($2.78$) at the low end of the range, around
125 minutes, falling to a nadir near 364 minutes --- a deficiency effect rather than a graded
dose--response.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Minutes awake, deep sleep and REM are monotone across their range, matching the
\code{linear} classification in Table~\ref{tab:res-spline}.



\subsection{Model diagnostics}
\label{sec:res-diag}
Every diagnostic passes across all 60 fitted models. GVIF never approaches the conventional
threshold of 5, so collinearity is not a concern even in the 15-model combined configurations.
Maximum Cook's distance is two orders of magnitude below 1, so no single participant drives any
estimate. Box--Tidwell $p$-values exceed $0.05$ for both continuous covariates everywhere, so
modelling age and BMI linearly on the logit scale is defensible. No analysis frame contains a
duplicated \code{person_id}, the specific join hazard identified in Section~\ref{sec:joinhazards}.

That the diagnostics are clean deserves emphasis rather than reassurance: none of the eight defects
in Section~\ref{sec:defects-run} is the kind regression diagnostics detect. A constant exposure, a
duplicated variable and a mis-specified wear proxy all produce perfectly well-behaved models. That
is precisely why they survived to this point, and why the distributional checks in
Table~\ref{tab:res-cut} did the work the diagnostics could not.

\subsection{Figures}
\label{sec:res-figs}
The run produced 68 figures: six forest plots of adjusted odds ratios and six GVIF plots, one pair
per configuration, and 56 spline curves with confidence bands, one per exposure--outcome pair. Each
renders a table reported above and introduces no additional results. The activity forest plots and
the 28 activity spline curves should not be circulated until the defects are resolved, since they
display the compromised exposures.


%======================================================================
\section{Pre-Specified Expectations and Interpretation Framework}
%======================================================================
Consistent with the FGID portfolio's hypothesis, we anticipate that higher sleep quantity and quality
and higher physical activity will be associated with \emph{lower} adjusted odds of both GERD and
oesophagitis, and that markers of fragmented sleep or high sedentary time will be associated with
\emph{higher} odds. An aOR of, say, $0.85$ for Q4 versus Q1 of \code{min_asleep_quartile} means that,
holding the adjustment set fixed, participants in the highest sleep-duration quartile have $15\%$ lower
odds of the outcome than those in the lowest. A monotone decline across
Q2$\rightarrow$Q3$\rightarrow$Q4 constitutes a dose--response pattern.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{4.6cm}p{2.6cm}p{5.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Exposure (high vs low)} & \textbf{Predicted aOR} & \textbf{Primary mechanism} \\
\midrule
More minutes asleep & $<1$ (protective) & More clearance, less arousal, lower sensitivity. \\
Higher sleep efficiency & $<1$ & Consolidated sleep, less supine low-clearance time. \\
More deep / REM sleep & $<1$ & Restorative sleep, autonomic balance. \\
More minutes awake / restless & $>1$ (harmful) & Fragmentation, sympathetic arousal. \\
More daily steps / active minutes & $<1$ & Weight control, vagal tone, motility. \\
More sedentary minutes & $>1$ & Adiposity, postprandial recumbency. \\
Higher corrected max HR & $<1$ (fitness proxy) & Autonomic/cardiovascular fitness. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Pre-specified directional predictions for the exposure--outcome associations (both outcomes).
Predictions are expected to be directionally similar for GERD and oesophagitis, with acid-contact
pathways potentially stronger for the erosive subset and perceptual pathways stronger for broad GERD.}
\end{table}

\subsection{A worked example of reading one output row}
Suppose the C1 (sleep, broad GERD) stacked table reports, for \code{min_asleep_quartile}, adjusted odds
ratios of $0.94$ (Q2), $0.88$ (Q3), and $0.82$ (Q4) versus Q1, with the Q4 95\% CI $(0.74, 0.91)$ and a
complete-case $N$ of, say, $18{,}500$. This would be read as follows: participants in the highest
quartile of average nightly sleep have about $18\%$ lower adjusted odds of a GERD phenotype than those in
the lowest quartile; the interval excludes $1$, so the association is statistically distinguishable from
the null at the conventional level; and the monotone decline $0.94 \rightarrow 0.88 \rightarrow 0.82$
across Q2--Q4 is a dose--response signature that strengthens (though cannot prove) a protective reading.
The same row for C2 (oesophagitis) might show a similar direction with a wider interval, reflecting the
smaller case count; a materially \emph{stronger} Q4 effect for oesophagitis than for broad GERD would
point toward an acid-contact-time pathway, whereas a weaker one would point toward a
symptom-perception pathway. The backward-model column indicates whether the estimate is stable when
non-contributing covariates are dropped, and the footnoted $N$ makes the complete-case sample explicit.
This single-row reading generalizes to every exposure in every configuration, because all six are
produced by the same engine.

Three internal-consistency checks structure interpretation. First, \emph{concordance across outcomes}:
if an exposure association is directionally consistent for broad GERD and for the erosive oesophagitis
subset, it is less likely to be an artifact of symptom-reporting behavior and more likely to reflect a
mucosal-injury pathway. Second, \emph{concordance across exposure domains}: agreement between the
sleep-only, activity-only, and mutually-adjusted combined estimates indicates that neither domain is
merely a proxy for the other. Third, \emph{temporal concordance}: agreement between the cross-sectional
primary estimate and the post-Fitbit temporal definition is the key guard against reverse causation.
Because the design is cross-sectional, the primary estimates remain associations, not causal effects.

%======================================================================
\section{Threats to Validity}
%======================================================================
Beyond the item-level limitations below, it is useful to organize the principal threats to validity by
epidemiologic type and to state how the design addresses each.

\subsection{Selection bias}
The cohort is restricted to participants who own a Fitbit, share it with \emph{All of Us}, wear it
enough to meet the coverage thresholds, and share EHR data. Each filter can associate with both
exposure and outcome (for example, health-motivated device use). The design cannot remove this but
makes it transparent through the CONSORT waterfall, and adjusts for socio-economic status, which is a
principal driver of device access.

\subsection{Information (measurement) bias}
The exposure is objective (device-recorded) but the outcome is EHR-coded and therefore subject to
detection and coding variation: participants with more health-care contact are both more likely to be
coded GERD and may differ in sleep and activity. Conditioning on the Charlson index and comorbidities
partially blocks this ``surveillance'' pathway. The $\geq 2$-record and $\geq 2$-distinct-date rules
reduce false-positive coding at the cost of sensitivity.

\subsection{Confounding}
Addressed by the pre-specified adjustment set (Section~9.2), the pre-Fitbit temporal covariate rule
(so covariates precede exposure), and the mutually-adjusted combined models (so each exposure domain is
adjusted for the other). Residual confounding from unmeasured factors (diet, meal timing, caffeine,
hiatal hernia) remains possible and is stated as a limitation.

\subsection{Reverse causation}
The most specific threat for reflux: nocturnal GERD fragments sleep, so a cross-sectional
sleep--GERD association could run outcome$\rightarrow$exposure. The post-Fitbit temporal outcome
(\code{has_post_fitbit_gerd}), which requires the first GERD code to appear well after the exposure
window, is the primary guard; concordance between it and the primary estimate is the key internal check.

\subsection{External validity}
Findings generalize to adult, EHR-sharing, Fitbit-wearing \emph{All of Us} participants with sufficient
wear time --- a group that is younger, more affluent, and more health-engaged than the general
population. Estimates should not be extrapolated to non-wearers without caution.

%======================================================================
\section{Limitations}
%======================================================================
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item \textbf{Cross-sectional design.} Temporality is only partially addressed; primary estimates
  cannot establish causation, and reflux itself disrupts sleep.
  \item \textbf{EHR phenotyping.} Diagnosis-code outcomes miss undiagnosed or over-the-counter-managed
  reflux and vary with coding practice; the $\geq 2$-record rule trades sensitivity for specificity.
  \item \textbf{Oesophagitis descendant scope.} The oesophagitis phenotype is the descendant set of
  concept 30753, which may include non-reflux causes (eosinophilic, infectious, pill-induced or
  radiation oesophagitis). Unless the seed is narrowed in the Cohort Builder, the outcome is
  ``oesophagitis'' rather than ``reflux oesophagitis'' specifically, and estimates should be read
  accordingly. The superseded approach of defining the erosive subset by concept-name
  match captures ``GERD with esophagitis'' but may miss erosive-esophagitis concepts coded outside the
  GERD hierarchy unless their seeds are added in the Cohort Builder.
  \item \textbf{Wearable selection.} Fitbit users are not representative of the general population.
  \item \textbf{Residual confounding.} Diet, meal timing, caffeine, body position, and hiatal hernia
  remain unmeasured; hiatal hernia would require a new condition pull.
  \item \textbf{Concept-set completeness.} GERD and drug-class concept IDs are seeds requiring Cohort
  Builder verification; the descendant-expansion design (Section~\ref{sec:subtlety}) mitigates but does
  not eliminate this.
  \item \textbf{Covariate-timing heterogeneity.} \code{has_pud} is loaded from a file computed on the
  activity-based first-Fitbit date; the small resulting timing difference is a minor, documented caveat.
\end{itemize}

%======================================================================
\section{Future Directions and Extensions}
%======================================================================
The suite is deliberately extensible. Several extensions follow naturally from the shared-engine design,
each requiring only a new modeling data frame or a new outcome column while reusing the same functions.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item \textbf{Dose--response modeling.} Replace the quartile factors with restricted cubic splines on
  the continuous metrics to estimate smooth exposure--response curves, using the Box--Tidwell result to
  decide where non-linearity matters.
  \item \textbf{Effect-measure modification.} Add interaction terms (for example, sleep $\times$ BMI, or
  sleep $\times$ sex) to test whether the reflux association differs across subgroups.
  \item \textbf{Erosive-severity gradient.} Extend the oesophagitis phenotype toward a graded outcome
  (reflux esophagitis, stricture, Barrett metaplasia) to test a monotone exposure--severity relationship.
  \item \textbf{Fatigue and symptom stratification.} Incorporate the fatigue-questionnaire data used in
  the sibling IBS work to stratify or adjust the sleep analyses.
  \item \textbf{Longitudinal follow-up.} Move from cross-sectional prevalence to incident GERD after the
  Fitbit window, strengthening the temporal argument beyond the current post-Fitbit sensitivity analysis.
  \item \textbf{Medication-response phenotyping.} Extend the treated-GERD flag to distinguish
  PPI-responsive from PPI-refractory disease, a clinically important axis of reflux heterogeneity.
  \item \textbf{Additional physiological exposures.} Heart-rate variability and resting heart rate, if
  available, would sharpen the autonomic-pathway hypothesis.
\end{itemize}
Because every extension plugs into \code{analyze_all_outcomes()} through the same helper, adding one does
not perturb the six primary configurations, preserving the internal consistency that the architecture is
designed to guarantee.

%======================================================================
\section{Integration Across the FGID Portfolio}
%======================================================================
This GERD/oesophagitis suite is the fourth member of a coordinated program that already covers irritable
bowel syndrome, functional dyspepsia, and constipation, each relating the same Fitbit-derived sleep and
activity exposures to a disorder of gut--brain interaction. Because the studies share eligibility rules,
exposure quartilization, covariate definitions, and (now) a common modeling engine, their estimates are
directly comparable. Table~\ref{tab:portfolio} places GERD within the portfolio.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{2.7cm}p{3.4cm}p{6.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Study} & \textbf{Outcome(s)} & \textbf{Distinctive features} \\
\midrule
IBS & \code{has_ibs} (+ subtypes) & Multinomial subtype models; severe-IBS medication subgroup. \\
Functional dyspepsia & \code{has_fd} (strict/broad) & Rome-based phenotypes; early-satiety extension. \\
Constipation & \code{has_constipation} & Simple binary outcome; heart-rate-correction activity analysis. \\
\textbf{GERD / oesophagitis} & \code{has_gerd_any}; \code{has_esophagitis} & Two acid-related phenotypes; PUD covariate; sleep, activity, and combined exposures under one shared engine. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The GERD/oesophagitis analysis within the functional-GI-disorder portfolio.}
\label{tab:portfolio}
\end{table}

Cross-disorder comparison is scientifically informative. If, for example, poor sleep is associated with
higher odds across all four disorders, that pattern supports a shared gut--brain mechanism rather than a
reflux-specific one; if the association is strongest for the erosive oesophagitis phenotype, it points to
an acid-contact pathway specific to the upper GI tract. The portfolio design --- identical exposures,
harmonized covariates, and a common engine --- is what makes such comparisons valid rather than
confounded by differing analytic choices. The GERD suite's two nested outcomes add a within-disorder
axis (symptom-coded vs erosive) that the other studies do not have, giving the portfolio its first
built-in objective-injury comparator.

%======================================================================
\section{Robustness, Triangulation, and Pre-Specification}
%======================================================================
The strength of the design is less in any single estimate than in the pattern of agreement across
deliberately varied analyses. This section states the pre-specified robustness and triangulation plan.

\subsection{Triangulation across three axes}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item \textbf{Across outcomes.} A sleep or activity association that holds for both broad GERD and the
  erosive oesophagitis subset is more credible than one that appears for only the symptom-coded outcome,
  because the erosive phenotype reflects objective mucosal injury.
  \item \textbf{Across exposure domains.} Agreement among the sleep-only (C1/C2), activity-only (C3/C4),
  and mutually-adjusted combined (C5/C6) estimates indicates that neither domain is merely a proxy for
  the other; the mutually-adjusted models are the decisive test of independent contribution.
  \item \textbf{Across case definitions.} Agreement between the primary (temporal $\geq 2$-record) definition and
  the stricter distinct-date, temporal, and treated-disease definitions indicates the association is not
  an artifact of a particular phenotype rule.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Pre-specification}
The exposures, the two outcomes, the adjustment set, the reference levels, the quartile
parameterization, the full-plus-backward modeling procedure, and the sensitivity analyses are all fixed
in code \emph{before} estimation, in the shared helper. Because the helper is the single source of these
choices, there is no opportunity for per-configuration analytic drift or outcome-dependent covariate
selection; the backward step is a parsimony check layered on top of a pre-specified full model, not a
data-driven search for the reported estimate.

\subsection{What would count as a robust finding}
A robust protective sleep association, for instance, would show: an aOR below $1$ for the top sleep
quartile in both C1 and C2; a monotone quartile gradient; survival of adjustment for activity in C5/C6;
concordance with the post-Fitbit temporal definition; and stability between the full and backward
models. A finding meeting only some of these criteria would be reported as suggestive and interpreted
with the corresponding caution. This graded standard, applied identically across all six configurations
by construction, is the payoff of the shared-engine architecture.

%======================================================================
\section{Conclusion}
%======================================================================
This report specifies a complete, reproducible suite relating objectively measured sleep and physical
activity to two nested EHR-defined outcomes --- broad GERD and erosive oesophagitis --- in \emph{All of
Us} CDR v9. Six configurations (two outcomes $\times$ three exposure sets) are executed by a single
shared modeling engine that guarantees statistical identity across the sleep, activity, and combined
analyses. The suite faithfully mirrors the project's IBS and constipation studies while correcting
their naming inconsistencies, removing IBS-only subtype machinery, adding GERD-appropriate
gastrointestinal covariates and a second erosive outcome, and extending the design to physical activity
and to mutually-adjusted combined models. Together with distinct-date, temporal, overlap, and
treated-disease sensitivity analyses, the design supports a robust, portfolio-consistent assessment of
whether sleep and activity are associated with reflux disease and its erosive form.

\section{Appendix C: Selected Code Excerpts}
%======================================================================

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={Two nested outcomes from one GERD pull (analysis files).}]
# Broad GERD: >=2 GERD records (computed over ALL rows of the GERD-only pull)
R9_gerd_status <- R9_gerd_outcome %>%
  group_by(person_id) %>% summarise(has_gerd = n() >= 2, .groups = "drop")

# Oesophagitis (erosive) subset: >=2 records whose concept name is esophagitis
R9_esophagitis_status <- R9_gerd_outcome %>%
  filter(grepl("esophagitis|oesophagitis", standard_concept_name, ignore.case = TRUE)) %>%
  group_by(person_id) %>% summarise(has_esophagitis = n() >= 2, .groups = "drop")
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={The shared driver: both outcomes, one exposure set.}]
source("GERD Analysis Helpers R9.R")
modeling_df <- prep_modeling_df(final_analysis_df, SLEEP_EXPOSURES)  # or ACTIVITY_EXPOSURES / union
# Runs descriptive + univariable + multivariable + diagnostics for BOTH
# has_gerd and has_esophagitis over every exposure:
results <- analyze_all_outcomes(modeling_df, exposures = SLEEP_EXPOSURES)
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={Per-exposure adjusted modeling inside the engine (abridged).}]
run_models_for_outcome <- function(df, outcome_col, outcome_labels, exposures, covars, ...) {
  d0 <- factor_outcome(df, outcome_col, outcome_labels)
  fit_one <- function(exposure) {
    d <- d0 %>% select(all_of(c(outcome_col, exposure, covars))) %>% drop_na()
    m_full <- glm(as.formula(paste0(outcome_col, " ~ ",
                  paste(c(exposure, covars), collapse = " + "))), d, family = binomial())
    m_step <- MASS::stepAIC(m_full, direction = "backward",
                  scope = list(lower = as.formula(paste0("~ ", exposure)),
                               upper = formula(m_full)), trace = FALSE)
    # AUC (pROC), VIF (car), gtsummary full-vs-backward merge ...
  }
  purrr::map(intersect(exposures, names(d0)), fit_one)
}
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={Combined mutually-adjusted model: sleep + activity together.}]
fit_combined_pair <- function(df, outcome_col, outcome_labels, sleep_expo, act_expo, covars) {
  d <- df; d[[outcome_col]] <- factor(d[[outcome_col]], c(FALSE, TRUE), labels = outcome_labels)
  d <- d %>% select(all_of(c(outcome_col, sleep_expo, act_expo, covars))) %>% drop_na()
  glm(as.formula(paste0(outcome_col, " ~ ", sleep_expo, " + ", act_expo, " + ",
      paste(covars, collapse = " + "))), d, family = binomial())   # each domain adjusts for the other
}
\end{lstlisting}

\section{Appendix D: Glossary of Key Derived Variables}
%======================================================================
\begin{longtable}{@{}p{5.2cm}p{8.3cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Variable} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\code{first_fitbit_sleep_date} & Earliest valid Fitbit sleep date; anchors sleep-file covariate windows. \\
\code{first_fitbit_date} & Earliest Fitbit date (activity file: activity stream; combined file: $\min$ of both streams). \\
\code{age_at_fitbit_start} / \code{age_cat} & Age and its category ($<$30, 30--44, 45--59, 60--74, 75+) at the first Fitbit date. \\
\code{n_valid_nights} / \code{n_valid_days} & Valid sleep nights / valid activity days (eligibility gates). \\
\code{avg_min_asleep}, \ldots, \code{avg_sleep_efficiency} & Participant-level mean of each nightly sleep metric. \\
\code{avg_daily_steps}, \code{avg_*_active_min}, \code{avg_sedentary_min} & Participant-level mean daily steps and activity-zone minutes. \\
\code{avg_daily_max_hr_minute_all_days} & Corrected mean of daily maximum heart rate. \\
\code{min_asleep_quartile}, \ldots & Cohort quartile (Q1 reference) of a sleep metric --- a modeled exposure. \\
\code{step_quartile}, \ldots, \code{max_hr_minute_all_days_quartile} & Cohort quartile of an activity metric --- a modeled exposure. \\
\code{has_gerd_no_eso} & Primary broad outcome: $\geq 2$ GERD records with the first $\geq 180$ days after the first Fitbit record. \\
\code{has_esophagitis} & Primary erosive outcome: $\geq 2$ esophagitis-named records. \\
\code{has_gerd_no_eso_dates} & Sensitivity: $\geq 2$ GERD records on distinct dates. \\
\code{has_post_fitbit_gerd} & Temporal sensitivity: first GERD $\geq 180$ days after first Fitbit, then $\geq 2$ codes. \\
\code{severe_gerd_binary} & Optional: GERD \emph{and} $\geq 2$ post-diagnosis PPI/$H_2$RA dates. \\
\code{has_pud} & GERD-specific gastrointestinal covariate. \\
\code{has_depression} \ldots \code{has_sleep_apnea} & Pre-Fitbit, $\geq 2$-distinct-date comorbidity indicators. \\
\code{on_beta_blocker} \ldots \code{on_hypnotics} & Sustained pre-exposure medication-use flags. \\
\code{cci_score} / \code{cci_cat} & Charlson Comorbidity Index, continuous and categorical (0, 1--2, 3--4, 5+). \\
\code{median_bmi} / \code{bmi_closest} & Within-person median BMI; nearest BMI within $\pm 180$ days. \\
\code{alcohol_likert_final} & Daily-drinking Likert (0 = none through 5 = $\geq 10$/day). \\
\code{smoking_binary} & Ever smoked $\geq 100$ cigarettes (1) vs not (0). \\
\code{education_collapsed}, \code{income_collapsed} & Collapsed socio-economic factors. \\
\code{final_analysis_sleep_gerd_df} & Sleep modeling frame (configs C1, C2). \\
\code{final_analysis_activity_gerd_df} & Activity modeling frame (configs C3, C4). \\
\code{final_analysis_activity_sleep_gerd_df} & Combined modeling frame (configs C5, C6). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

%======================================================================
\section{Appendix F: Per-File Walkthrough}
%======================================================================
This appendix summarizes, step by step, what each of the six deliverable scripts does.

\subsection{\code{GERD Outcome Data Upload R9.R}}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=0pt]
  \item Pins the workspace CDR to v9 and constructs the \code{cb_criteria} descendant-expansion SQL for
  seeds 4144111 and 30753 in two separate pulls, restricted to Fitbit-sharers.
  \item Exports the query to the workspace bucket, reads it back, and previews the captured GERD
  concepts.
  \item Flags the oesophagitis subset by concept-name match and prints its size.
  \item Saves \code{R9_gerd_outcome.rds} (with the \code{is_esophagitis} flag).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{\code{GERD Analysis Helpers R9.R}}
Defines the outcome objects, exposure lists, adjustment covariates, and label lookup, and the functions
\code{prep_modeling_df()}, \code{descriptive_table()}, \code{univariate_table()},
\code{run_models_for_outcome()}, \code{stack_results()}, \code{run_diagnostics()}, and
\code{analyze_all_outcomes()}. It installs any missing packages on load and is sourced by all three
analysis files.

\subsection{\code{Sleep and GERD Analysis File R9.R}}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=0pt]
  \item Loads R9 inputs; computes the first sleep date and age; cleans sleep records; builds
  participant-level sleep summaries.
  \item Builds both outcomes (\code{has_gerd_no_eso}, \code{has_esophagitis}) and the sensitivity definitions.
  \item Builds behavioral, comorbidity, PUD, IBS, Charlson, BMI, medication, and socio-economic
  covariates on the sleep anchor.
  \item Applies eligibility, assembles \code{R9_final_analysis_sleep_gerd_df}, creates integer sleep
  quartiles, sources the helper, and calls \code{analyze_all_outcomes()} for both outcomes.
  \item Runs the optional treated-GERD block if the drug file exists.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{\code{Activity and GERD Analysis File R9.R}}
Parallel structure on the activity stream: first activity date and age; steps, activity-zone, wear, and
corrected max-HR summaries; the same outcomes and covariates on the activity anchor; the
$\geq 30$-valid-day eligibility; \code{R9_final_analysis_activity_gerd_df}; integer activity quartiles;
and \code{analyze_all_outcomes()} for both outcomes.

\subsection{\code{Activity Sleep and GERD Analysis File R9.R}}
Builds both exposure streams, anchors covariates to the earliest Fitbit date, forms the intersection
cohort, assembles \code{R9_final_analysis_activity_sleep_gerd_df} with both quartile sets, runs
\code{analyze_all_outcomes()} over the union of exposures, and then fits the four mutually-adjusted
sleep$\times$activity pairs for both outcomes.

\subsection{\code{GERD Pharmacologics Data Upload.R}}
Pulls PPI (ancestor 21600095) and $H_2$RA (ancestor 21600096) exposures via the \code{concept_ancestor}
double-join for Fitbit-sharers and saves \code{R9_gerd_drug_df.rds}, enabling the treated-GERD
sensitivity analysis.

\section{Appendix H: Helper Function Reference}
%======================================================================
The shared engine (\code{GERD Analysis Helpers R9.R}) exposes the following objects and functions. This
reference documents each one's role, inputs, and outputs.

\begin{longtable}{@{}p{4.9cm}p{8.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Object / function} & \textbf{Role, inputs, and output} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\code{GERD_OUTCOMES} & Named list of the two outcomes; each has \code{col} (the data-frame column) and
\code{labels} (reference, case). Drives the both-outcome loop. \\
\code{SLEEP_EXPOSURES} & Character vector of the eight sleep quartile column names. \\
\code{ACTIVITY_EXPOSURES} & Character vector of the seven activity quartile column names. \\
\code{GERD_BINARY_COVARS} & Binary covariates shown as No/Yes and used as 0/1 predictors. \\
\code{GERD_LABELS} & Named character vector mapping variable names to display labels. \\
\code{GERD_ADJ_COVARS} & The adjustment covariate vector (see the Covariates section). \\
\code{prep_modeling_df(df, exposures)} & Input: a final analysis frame and its exposure names. Builds
collapsed factor covariates and relevels quartiles to Q1--Q4 (Q1 reference). Output: the same frame with
model-ready factors. \\
\code{descriptive_table(df, outcome_col, outcome_labels, exposures, extra_cont)} & Builds a
\code{tbl_summary} stratified by the outcome with chi-square tests and adjusted $p$-values. \\
\code{univariate_table(df, outcome_col, outcome_labels, exposures)} & Unadjusted ORs for exposures and
covariates via \code{tbl_uvregression}. \\
\code{run_models_for_outcome(df, outcome_col, outcome_labels, exposures, covars, cci_label)} & Fits, per
exposure, a full model and a backward-AIC model (exposure forced in); returns a named list with $N$, AUC,
VIF, merged tables, and quick exposure rows. \\
\code{stack_results(results)} & Stacks the per-exposure merged tables into one \code{tbl_stack}. \\
\code{run_diagnostics(results, modeling_df, outcome_col, adj_covars)} & Runs the five assumption checks. \\
\code{analyze_all_outcomes(modeling_df, exposures, covars, cci_label, extra_cont)} & Driver: loops both
outcomes, calling the four routines above; returns a nested list keyed by outcome. \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

%======================================================================
\section{Appendix I: Data Dictionary of the Final Analysis Frames}
%======================================================================
The three \code{R9_final_analysis_*_gerd_df.rds} frames are one row per participant. They share the
covariate and outcome columns below; they differ only in which exposure block is present (sleep,
activity, or both).

\begin{longtable}{@{}p{4.4cm}p{2.4cm}p{6.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Column(s)} & \textbf{Type} & \textbf{Description} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\code{person_id} & chr & Participant identifier (unique per row). \\
\code{has_gerd_no_eso}, \code{has_esophagitis} & logical & The two primary outcomes. \\
\code{has_post_fitbit_gerd}, \code{severe_gerd_binary} & logical & Sensitivity outcomes (sleep file). \\
\code{min_*_quartile} (8) & int/factor & Sleep exposures (sleep and combined frames). \\
\code{step_quartile}, \code{*_active_quartile}, \code{sedentary_quartile}, \code{max_hr_*_quartile} & int/factor & Activity exposures (activity and combined frames). \\
\code{avg_min_*}, \code{avg_*_active_min}, \code{avg_daily_steps}, \code{avg_sedentary_min} & num & Continuous exposure means. \\
\code{n_valid_nights}, \code{n_valid_days} & int & Coverage counts. \\
\code{age_at_fitbit_start}, \code{age_cat} & num/factor & Age and category. \\
\code{race}, \code{ethnicity}, \code{sex_at_birth} & chr & Raw demographics (collapsed in the engine). \\
\code{education_response}, \code{income_response} & chr & Raw SES survey responses. \\
\code{alcohol_likert_final}, \code{smoking_binary} & num & Behavioral covariates. \\
\code{median_bmi} & num & Median BMI covariate. \\
\code{cci_score}, \code{cci_cat} & int/factor & Charlson index (continuous and categorical). \\
\code{has_pud}, \code{has_ibs} & logical & GI covariates. \\
\code{has_depression} \ldots \code{has_sleep_apnea} & logical & Comorbidity covariates. \\
\code{on_beta_blocker} \ldots \code{on_hypnotics} & logical & Medication-use covariates. \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\section{Appendix J: Software and Package Reference}
%======================================================================
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.0cm}p{10.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Package} & \textbf{Role in the pipeline} \\
\midrule
\code{tidyverse} & Data manipulation (\code{dplyr}, \code{tidyr}, \code{purrr}, \code{stringr}, \code{forcats}, \code{ggplot2}). \\
\code{lubridate} & Date handling for temporal covariate and BMI windows. \\
\code{rlang} & Tidy-evaluation helpers (dynamic outcome/column injection in the engine). \\
\code{gtsummary} & Descriptive (\code{tbl_summary}), univariable (\code{tbl_uvregression}), and adjusted (\code{tbl_regression}, \code{tbl_merge}, \code{tbl_stack}) tables with adjusted $p$-values. \\
\code{broom} & Tidies model output into data frames (quick exposure-row extraction). \\
\code{MASS} & Backward AIC selection (\code{stepAIC}). \\
\code{car} & Generalized variance inflation factors (\code{vif}). \\
\code{pROC} & ROC AUC (discrimination). \\
\code{bigrquery} & BigQuery execution for the outcome and drug pulls. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{R packages and their roles. The shared helper installs any that are missing on first load.}
\end{table}

%======================================================================
\section{Appendix K: Glossary of Statistical Terms}
%======================================================================
\begin{longtable}{@{}p{4.4cm}p{9.1cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Term} & \textbf{Definition as used here} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Odds & $p/(1-p)$ for outcome probability $p$. \\
Odds ratio (OR) & Ratio of odds between two covariate settings; $1$ = no association. \\
Adjusted OR (aOR) & OR from a multivariable model, holding covariates fixed. \\
Logit & Log-odds, $\log\{p/(1-p)\}$; the link function of logistic regression. \\
Reference level & The category (e.g. Q1) against which other levels' ORs are computed. \\
Quartile & One of four equal-frequency groups (\code{ntile(x,4)}); Q1 lowest, Q4 highest. \\
Maximum likelihood & Estimation by maximizing the probability of the observed data. \\
Wald confidence interval & $\exp(\hat\beta \pm 1.96\,\mathrm{SE})$ on the OR scale. \\
Complete-case analysis & Fitting on rows with no missing model variable; the $N$ is reported per model. \\
AIC & Akaike Information Criterion; balances fit and parameter count in backward selection. \\
Backward selection & Iterative covariate removal by AIC, with the exposure forced to remain. \\
AUC / $c$-statistic & Area under the ROC curve; probability a case outranks a non-case (0.5 = chance). \\
VIF / GVIF & (Generalized) variance inflation factor; multicollinearity diagnostic. \\
Perfect separation & A covariate perfectly predicts the outcome; flagged by very large standard errors. \\
Cook's distance & Influence measure; values above $4/N$ flag potentially influential rows. \\
Box--Tidwell test & Tests linearity of a continuous covariate in the logit via an $x\log x$ term. \\
FDR $q$-value & Benjamini--Hochberg false-discovery-rate analogue of a $p$-value under multiplicity. \\
Confounder & A common cause of exposure and outcome; adjusted for. \\
Mediator & A variable on the causal path from exposure to outcome; not adjusted for (over-adjustment). \\
Collider & A common effect of two variables; conditioning on it induces spurious association. \\
Reverse causation & Outcome influences the measured exposure; addressed by the temporal outcome. \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\vspace{1em}
\noindent\rule{\linewidth}{0.4pt}\\
{\footnotesize \emph{End of report.} This document describes code and methodology only; it contains no
individual-level \emph{All of Us} data.}


%======================================================================
\section{Appendix L: Design History and Protocol Amendments}
%======================================================================
The body of this report describes the study as it now stands. This appendix
records how the specification changed during development, so that a reader
comparing against an earlier draft, or against output files produced before a
change, can see what moved and why. Nothing here is current methodology; it is
provenance.

\subsection{Outcome definition, amended twice}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Original.} \code{has_gerd_no_eso} seeded on concept
\textbf{4144111} (``GERD without oesophagitis''), pulled in parallel with
oesophagitis (\textbf{30753}). Abandoned once the Cohort Builder showed
substantial overlap between participants carrying 4144111 and participants
carrying oesophagitis codes: participants accumulate both kinds of code over
time, so the concept's label does not identify a non-oesophagitic group.
\item \textbf{Intermediate.} Broad GERD (\textbf{318800}) with a
``GERD without oesophagitis'' group derived by excluding oesophagitis carriers,
giving three modelled outcomes and nine configurations.
\item \textbf{Current.} Broad GERD and oesophagitis modelled directly, as two
outcomes over three exposure sets. The derived non-oesophagitic flag is retained
descriptively but is not modelled, does not appear in any table, and consumes no
multiplicity budget.
\end{enumerate}

\noindent Output files carrying a \code{gerd_no_eso} prefix are from a run under
design~2 and should be deleted before re-running.

\subsection{Data source, amended}
The study originally assembled its analysis frames from the project's existing
\code{.rds} objects, which are the same objects behind the published IBS paper.
It now builds everything from the three Workbench datasets described in the Data
Sources section. The two studies therefore share no denominator, and a GERD
number cannot inherit an IBS-era eligibility decision.

\subsection{Outcome pull, amended}
The original pull queried the \emph{All of Us} production CDR project directly
and exported through Cloud Storage. In the migrated workspace that project lies
outside the VPC Service Controls perimeter and every request failed. The
replacement queries the BigQuery dataset attached to the workspace as a
Resource, which is inside the perimeter, and downloads directly.

\subsection{Confounding control, amended on expert review}
Sleep medication, obstructive sleep apnoea and opioid analgesics were added to
the adjustment set; foregut surgery and upper gastrointestinal cancer became
exclusions rather than covariates; \code{has_ibs} was dropped as constant in
this concept set; and the partial comorbidity index was removed from the default
adjustment set because it double-counts diabetes and peptic ulcer disease. Each
change and its rationale is documented in the Covariates and Cohort Construction
sections.

\subsection{Analysis, extended}
The restricted cubic spline analysis and the quartile-versus-spline comparison
were added after the primary quartile analysis was complete. Both are additive:
no existing analysis file was modified, and the quartile results are unchanged by
their presence.
